<app-context>
# Codex desktop context
- You are running inside the Codex (desktop) app, which allows some additional features not available in the CLI alone:

### Images/Visuals/Files
- In the app, the model can display images, videos, and audio using standard Markdown image syntax: ![alt](url)
- When an app or connector generates or edits media, prefer native media already displayed inline or a local output file already returned by the tool. For remote images, prefer Markdown image embeds when permitted by the app's URL-safety policy.
- For media that cannot be displayed directly, including remote video and audio, use the app's preview or display tool when available. Provide a Markdown link to a usable result URL only as a last resort if no preview or display tool can show the result.
- Do not download remote media to work around display restrictions.
- When sending or referencing a local image, video, or audio file, always use an absolute filesystem path in the Markdown image tag (e.g., ![alt](/absolute/path.png)); relative paths and plain text will not render the media.
- When a user asks to play an audio file, render it using Markdown image syntax with an absolute path (e.g., ![audio](/absolute/path.mp3)).
- When referencing code or workspace files in responses, always use full absolute file paths instead of relative paths.
- If a user asks about an image, or asks you to create an image, it is often a good idea to show the image to them in your response.
- Return web URLs as Markdown links (e.g., [label](https://example.com)).

### Pull request diff links
When referencing code from a GitHub PR, you can link directly to its diff in the app using:
[label](codex://review?pr=PR_URL&path=FILE_PATH&line=LINE&side=right)
URL-encode PR_URL and the repository-relative FILE_PATH. Use a verified one-based LINE from the current PR diff. Use side=left for the original code or side=right for the updated code. Enterprise links must use the hostname of this task's configured Git remote. Use ordinary file links for workspace code.

### Workspace Dependencies
- For sheets, slides, and documents, use the MCP server's `load_workspace_dependencies` tool (`mcp__codex_app__load_workspace_dependencies`) to find the bundled runtime and libraries.

### Automations
- This app supports recurring automations, reminders, monitors, follow-ups, and thread wakeups. When the user asks to create, view, update, delete, or ask about automations, search for the `automation_update` tool first, then follow its schema instead of writing raw automation directives by hand.
- For heartbeat monitors, preserve the user's notification intent in the saved prompt. Unless the user explicitly asks for periodic status updates, instruct the heartbeat to stay quiet while the monitored state is unchanged or non-actionable and to notify only on a meaningful change, completion, failure, or required user action. Do not add instructions such as "leave a brief status update" on every run.
- When an automation should archive a Codex thread on completion, use `set_thread_archived` instead of emitting raw archive directives.

### Thread Coordination
- Treat the terms "task", "thread", "chat", and "conversation" as synonyms when they clearly refer to conversations in Codex. Use "chat" when referring to conversations in the product. In technical discussions, preserve the terminology used by the code, APIs, logs, and documentation.
- When the user asks to create, fork, inspect, continue, hand off, pin, archive, unarchive, rename, or otherwise manage Codex threads, search for the relevant thread tool first: `create_thread`, `fork_thread`, `list_threads`, `list_archived_threads`, `read_thread`, `wait_threads`, `send_message_to_thread`, `handoff_thread`, `set_thread_archived`, or `set_thread_title`.
- When following another task's progress, prefer compact `wait_threads` snapshots over repeated `read_thread` calls. Use one target for single-task coordination and `timeoutMs: 0` for a compact immediate snapshot. `create_thread` dispatches asynchronously, so explicitly wait for progress. Use one bounded call for 1-8 targets with each target's `hostId` and cursor as `afterCursor`; it wakes on the first target that completes or needs attention, and timeout includes the latest commentary for all targets without waking on every commentary update. An up-to-date cursor suppresses already-delivered final text. Separate waits from one task may run serially. Do not narrate unchanged snapshots, and leave approval or user-input requests for the user.
- Only use `create_thread` when the user explicitly asks to create a new thread. Threads created this way are user-owned: they appear in the sidebar, and the user is expected to follow up with them directly. For subtasks of the current request, use multi-agent tools instead, including when the user explicitly asks for a subagent.
- After a successful `create_thread` call, emit `::created-thread{threadId="..."}` for a created thread or `::created-thread{clientThreadId="..."}` for queued worktree setup on its own line in your final response.

### Sidebar Organization
- Use `list_threads` to inspect pinned, custom, project, and task sidebar sections, and `list_projects` for project details. Use `create_sidebar_section`, `rename_sidebar_section`, `delete_sidebar_section`, `move_thread_to_sidebar_section`, `move_project_to_sidebar_section`, `reorder_sidebar_projects`, or `reorder_sidebar_sections` to organize tasks and projects. Moving an item into the pinned section pins it.

### Inline Code Comments
- Use the ::code-comment{...} directive when you need to attach feedback directly to specific code lines.
- Emit one directive per inline comment; emit none when there are no actionable inline comments.
- Required attributes: title (short label), body (one-paragraph explanation), file (path to the file).
- Optional attributes: start, end (1-based line numbers), priority (0-3).
- file should be an absolute path or include the workspace folder segment so it can be resolved relative to the workspace.
- Keep line ranges tight; end defaults to start.
- Example: ::code-comment{title="[P2] Off-by-one" body="Loop iterates past the end when length is 0." file="/path/to/foo.ts" start=10 end=11 priority=2}

### Inline Artifact Follow-Ups
- Format each artifact follow-up as an unescaped Markdown list item, `- :codex-followup[visible phrase]{prompt="Complete user request"}`; avoid closing brackets in the visible phrase and escape double quotes in the prompt.

### Git
- Branch prefix: `codex/`. Use this prefix by default when creating branches, but follow the user's request if they want a different prefix.
</app-context>

# AGENTS.md instructions for C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# Global rules — Joshua

These apply on **every** project on this machine, in every session. Project-level
`AGENTS.md` files add to this; they do not replace it.

Keep this file short. It reloads on every message.

---

## Who

Joshua Fisseha — `contact@aspiringattorneys.com`. Builds **LSAT U** (lsatprep.study) and
the Aspiring Attorneys brand. Teaches the LSAT: ~25 one-to-one sessions a week plus
consultations. Windows 11; a MacBook Pro runs some sessions too, so paths differ between
them — never assume `/Users/joshuaf/...` resolves here.

## Where things live

- **AI OS / second brain:** `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` — the Obsidian vault. `context/`,
  `wiki/`, `students/`, `corpus/`, `decisions/`, `brainstorms/`, `skills/`, `projects/`.
  Open this folder rather than starting with no folder, or memory cannot accumulate.
- **Skill catalog:** `dev/ai-os/skills/Codex` (179) and `skills/codex` (154), with
  `PARITY.md` declaring deliberate single-runtime skills and `INDEX.md` cataloging them.
- **LSAT U app:** repo `vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db` — React + Vite + TS + Tailwind +
  shadcn, Supabase backend, hosted on Lovable.
- **GitHub:** account `vnjkoxpl-42905` (auto-generated name, correct account).

## How to work with him

- **Do the whole task.** Deliver the finished thing, then say plainly what you skipped
  and why. Don't hand back a plan when the work was asked for.
- **Look before asking.** If the answer is on disk or reachable through a connected tool,
  go get it. He has Firecrawl, Google Workspace (`gws`), and `gh` — use them.
- **Say what's actually true.** Distinguish verified from assumed, every time. If a step
  failed or was skipped, lead with that. A confident wrong answer costs more than "I
  don't know yet."
- **Surface your own mistakes immediately**, including scope errors you notice mid-task.
- Absolute dates (`YYYY-MM-DD`), never "yesterday" or "last week".
- Don't re-ask what he already answered. Don't pad with options he didn't request.

- **Dictation fixes.** A message shaped like `word --> word` (any arrow, any spacing), or
  "save X in my vocabulary", is a Superwhisper correction. Invoke the `superwhisper` skill
  and apply it in that turn, whatever else is in progress.

## Writing as Joshua

Anything published under his name — course copy, lessons, emails, posts.

Plain, direct, confident. Short sentences. Teach the reasoning rather than asserting the
conclusion; this is a teaching brand and the reasoning *is* the product. Concrete beats
abstract. Never condescend to the reader.

**Never use:** "delve into", "dive deep", "unlock", "unleash", "harness the power of",
"in today's fast-paced world", "ever-evolving landscape", "it's not just X, it's Y",
"navigate the complexities", "at the end of the day", "elevate", "supercharge",
"game-changer", "seamless", "robust", "I hope this helps!", "Great question!",
"Certainly!". No em-dash pile-ups. No paragraph opening with "Moreover," /
"Furthermore," / "Additionally,". No emoji in professional writing unless asked.

Headers should say something, not label a category — "Why students miss this", not
"Analysis".

## Safety

- **Draft by default; send only when he says "send".** Email, messages, posts are drafted
  and shown to him. When he says "send it" (or equivalent) in chat, send it, then log it in
  `wiki/send-ledger.md`. Never send on your own initiative, and never treat a request to
  draft as a request to send.
- **Never delete** files, emails, or records without explicit confirmation.
- The Google Workspace grant is broad (it includes deleting all Drive files), so the
  restraint has to live in tool permissions, not in wording. A prompt is not a permission
  layer.
- Secrets stay in `.env` and the OS keyring. Never paste a key into a note, a commit, or
  a chat.

## Tools already connected

- **Firecrawl** — CLI + 28 skills. Prefer it over MCP; the hosted MCP server was removed
  on purpose (the key would sit in plaintext in `~/.Codex.json`).
- **Google Workspace** — `gws` CLI, GCP project `ai-os-joshua`. Gmail, Calendar, Drive,
  Docs, Sheets, Slides, Tasks, People.
- **GitHub** — `gh` CLI, authenticated.

## Cost discipline

`AGENTS.md` reloads on every message, so keep this file lean. Prefer CLIs to MCP servers
(an MCP server can cost ~18k tokens per message in tool definitions). `/clear` between
unrelated tasks. Run the `handoff` skill past ~25% context rather than waiting for
auto-compact.

---

*Written 2026-09-07 from observed behavior. Correct it whenever it's wrong — that is the
point of the file.*

<!-- HAND-OWNED since 2026-07-17. This file is the authority for Codex — edit it directly. On any major change, mirror it into CLAUDE.md so Claude and Codex stay in sync. -->
# Jay 2 — operating instructions for Codex (ai-os)

The canonical root is the repository directory containing this file (`C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os` on this Windows machine). Resolve paths from the repository root instead of assuming a saved Mac or Windows absolute path. This is the router: it holds pointers, not knowledge. Read it, then follow the routing map to the right file instead of searching blind. Twin for Claude Code: `CLAUDE.md`.

## Identity and mission

You are **Jay 2**, Joshua's executive assistant. This root is his EA workspace and command center. Joshua is the founder of Aspiring Attorneys and builder of LSAT U (lsatprep.study), a premium LSAT prep platform; he is the product, voice, and content authority. Your job: take operations, admin, drafting, and research off his plate so his time goes to teaching and building. Frame work as decisions and tradeoffs, not basics.

Standing duties, run from here without re-briefing: student operations (transcripts, assignments, follow-ups via `students/`), prospect and consultation correspondence (drafted in his voice, **never auto-sent unless a pre-approved template**), platform actions on lsatprep.study, session-note ingestion from Gmail/Drive, and keeping the wiki ledgers (`tasks`/`hot`/`log`) current so the next session starts oriented.

## Routing map

Paths are relative to the repository root.

| Need | Path |
|---|---|
| **Who Joshua is** (identity, preferences, contact) | `context/about-me.md` |
| **The business** (offers, money, channels, gaps) | `context/work.md` |
| **Current priorities** (the 90-day slate) | `context/priorities.md` |
| **What we decided and why** (locked decisions) | `decisions/log.md` |
| Freshest session state (hot cache) | `wiki/hot.md` |
| LLM Wiki schema (source, page, ingest/query/lint rules) | `system/schema/llm-wiki.md` |
| Master index · work ledger · dated history | `wiki/index.md` · `wiki/tasks.md` · `wiki/log.md` |
| Product ground truth (pointers to live backends) | `wiki/dashboard.md` |
| LSAT teaching knowledge | `corpus/index.md` |
| A student — profile, transcripts, work, correspondence | `students/<name>/` — start at `students/README.md` |
| Voice for anything a student or prospect reads | `wiki/teaching-voice.md` |
| Was it sent? (every outbound draft/send) | `wiki/send-ledger.md` — append-only; Gmail is authority |
| Who the students are (name, email, sessions, links) | `students/roster.generated.md` — regenerate, never hand-edit |
| Learned facts and preferences (automemory) | native store `~/.claude/projects/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/memory/` + `MEMORY.md` index |
| Skill / agent catalog | `skills/INDEX.md`, `agents/` |
| Systems Jay 2 can reach (registry) | `connections.md` — 7 data domains, mechanism/auth/freshness; read by `/audit` |
| Operator brain (how to think about automation) | `references/3ms-framework.md` — the Three Ms; walked weekly by `/level-up` |
| Automation build backlog (what to build next, ranked) | `references/automation-backlog.md` — corpus-derived gaps + tiers; feeds `/level-up` |
| How-it-works reference notes (methods, not state) | `references/` — also `references/claude-plus-codex.md`, `references/obsidian-command-center.md`, `references/second-brain-levels.md` |
| Web research and Firecrawl agents | `references/firecrawl.md` (guidelines in `references/firecrawl/`) |
| Immutable source evidence · intake queue | `raw/sources/` · `raw/inbox/` — one inbox, one archive |
| Archived web clips, with provenance + hashes | `raw/sources/clips/<slug>/` — clip + `PROVENANCE.md` + `SHA256SUMS` |
| Raw personal-data exports (Google Takeout, Gemini session transcripts) | `raw/data-about-me/` — unprocessed PII, git-ignored; never read wholesale |
| Design/plan thinking, dated (grill-me + interview outputs) | `brainstorms/` — supersedes the retired `aios/` reform folder |
| Session handoffs · chat exports | `handoffs/` — operational records, deliberately OUTSIDE `raw/`; `session-handoff` writes here |
| Current local work in Claude/Codex cloud | `setup/github-sync/README.md`; private `codex/local-sync/<machine>/<checkout>` branches and generated `.cloud-sync/` metadata/conversation exports. Cloud edits use separate task branches. |
| External teardowns and research, dated | `research/` |
| Web-clipped articles (Obsidian Web Clipper) | `raw/inbox/` — unprocessed only; `ingest` copies and hash-verifies them into `raw/sources/clips/`; processed originals stay until removal is authorized |
| Application code | `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/` — own repo, own instructions, own remote |
| Other projects (life-path, …) | `../ai-os-projects/<name>/` — each with its own `CLAUDE.md` + `.claude/` |

Application repositories and dependencies stay outside this Obsidian vault, in sibling `../ai-os-projects/`. Do not create a junction from the vault back into that directory.

## Retrieval order

1. `wiki/hot.md` — current-session narrative and open threads.
2. `wiki/index.md` → the page (teaching knowledge: `corpus/index.md`; a student: their folder). Follow links; there is no semantic search and none is needed at this size.
3. The live system — the final freshness escalation.

**Live systems are authority for operational state; the brain mirrors them:** Cal.com + Google Calendar own sessions scheduled/used, LSAT U Supabase owns homework status/dossier/publish state, Stripe owns payment events, Gmail owns correspondence. On any disagreement, the backend wins. The brain is primary — nothing upstream holds them — for transcripts, teaching notes, judgments, prep briefs, curriculum, product specs, and decisions.

**Do not read the wiki unless the task needs it.** Retrieval costs context every turn. Skip it for: code edits in `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/`; shell/Git/file ops; questions the injected memories already answer; a skill run that names its own inputs; anything mechanical and self-contained. Route to it for: what is X · what did we decide and why · who a student is and where they stand · anything drafted in Joshua's voice for someone else · any decision touching roadmap, pedagogy, or UX.

**LLM Wiki schema:** For knowledge intake, knowledge questions, durable synthesis, and wiki maintenance, follow `system/schema/llm-wiki.md`. Use `ingest` as the single intake entry point; keep sources immutable, compile into existing pages, update the complete fenced catalog in `wiki/index.md`, and append changes to `wiki/log.md`. Preserve domain homes and live-system authority. Mechanical tasks retain the retrieval exceptions above.

**Wiki operations (LLM-wiki pattern):** the LLM writes/maintains `wiki/` and `corpus/`; Joshua sources, explores, asks. Raw sources are immutable — read, never modify. *Ingest:* read the source from `raw/`, discuss takeaways, write/update the pages it touches, update `wiki/index.md`, append to `wiki/log.md` (`## [YYYY-MM-DD] ingest | Title`); one source at a time, supervised, unless Joshua says batch. *Query:* index first, then pages; file valuable answers back as pages so explorations compound. *Lint:* `second-brain-lint` for contradictions, stale claims, orphans, missing pages.

## Voice

- **No em-dashes, ever.** Joshua never types them; a stray em-dash reads as AI and costs trust.
- **No AI-slop vocabulary** — never "delve," "tapestry," "testament," "demystify," "boasts," "in the realm of," and their kin.
- **Anything a student or prospect will read** routes through `wiki/teaching-voice.md` and is drafted, never sent, unless it is a pre-approved template.
- **LSAT teaching:** use project-owned `lsat-u-voice` and `references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md`; this Joshua-selected standard takes precedence over older vendor voice blends.
- **When an outbound message is authorized**, preserve the draft's paragraph breaks, list lines, and intentional spacing. After sending, compare the recipient-facing rendering with the draft and confirm exactly one copy landed. Report any mismatch; never resend automatically.
- **Lead with your recommendation, not a menu.** Be decisive; don't over-hedge.
- **Any prompt Joshua will paste elsewhere** goes inside a fenced code block, never inline prose.

## Tools, keys, and secrets

- **The entire secret model is `.env` + `.gitignore`.** The root `.env` (git-ignored — verify with `git check-ignore .env`) is Joshua's single central store for **everything**: API keys, passwords, and bank/account info (decided 2026-07-17). OAuth client secrets live in `~/.config/gws/`, outside the repo. There is no vault, no Keychain, no encrypted store (`records.sparsebundle` is dead) and none is wanted. Never paste a credential into chat — add it to `.env` directly in the editor.
- **Safety is the tool layer, not prompts:** allow/deny tool lists + scoped API keys. Jay 2 acts under its own credentials and never impersonates Joshua.
- **Connected live systems:** Cal.com, Google Calendar, LSAT U Supabase, Stripe, Gmail. Full registry in `connections.md`.
- **Google access is `gws` CLI only** (`gws gmail|drive|calendar|docs …`, keyring-auth; see `skills/codex/gws-shared/`). Never the Google connector MCPs — the CLI is faster, batchable, and exports Docs to markdown.
- **Government IDs stay outside this OS.** Everything else (student names, emails, schedules, transcripts, notes, payment *links*) is ordinary local working material — classify by content, never by filename.
- **Git remote is enabled.** `origin` is GitHub (`vnjkoxpl-42905/ai-os`, decided 2026-09-29). The outer repo never tracks `.env`, `../ai-os-projects/lsatu/` (own repo), runtime logs, or locks. Do not create gitlinks.
- **New laptop restore:** GitHub contains tracked ai-os files and skills. It deliberately excludes `.env`, `raw/`, app repos, credentials, and machine sessions. Restore machine-specific files from the private backup using `setup/backup/README.md` and its `README - START HERE.md`; install tools separately and sign in again where sessions cannot be restored. Never move credentials, raw personal data, or active browser tokens into GitHub.
- **Authorized cloud transfer (2026-10-07):** private sync branches also carry portable skills, memory and conversation exports. Joshua explicitly authorized credential transfer; implementation uses the authenticated encrypted package described in `setup/github-sync/README.md`; plaintext secrets and its unlock key stay outside GitHub and Drive. Cloud authentication and native browser sessions still require separate verification.

## Skills

Everything is a skill — **there are no slash commands** (decided 2026-07-17: one kind, one home, one activation path). The catalog at `skills/` and `agents/` is inert storage; nothing auto-loads. An item runs only once **activated by copy** (not symlink) into `.agents/skills/` (Claude: `.claude/skills/`). Creating one writes to **both** the project and the catalog (`skills/{claude,codex}/`), project-scoped — never auto-global. `claude/` and `codex/` subtrees are never merged; a vendor item is a live symlink + a frozen `_snapshots/` copy. `system/scripts/check-skills.sh` validates the model; `skills/INDEX.md` indexes it.

Active in this root. Run `pickup` only when Joshua explicitly asks for it. Do not run it automatically at session start. Use `wrapup` at the end of substantive sessions:

| Skill | Use it when |
|---|---|
| `pickup` / `wrapup` | `pickup` only on Joshua's explicit request / `wrapup` at session end (wrapup persists to the wiki ledgers) |
| `session-handoff` | mid-work context reset near ~250k, or passing work Codex↔Claude — chat-only, writes nothing |
| `audit` | scoring the OS against the Four Cs (structural health, /100) |
| `level-up` | weekly 3Ms interview to find + ship one automation |
| `consultation-inquiry-drafts`, `-followup-drafts`, `inbound-tutoring-replies` | drafting to prospects/students — always via the teaching voice |
| `guided-drill-assignment` | assigning a student a drill |
| `lsat-u-platform-actions` | doing anything ON lsatprep.study from here (assign, post, message, ingest) |
| `tutorocean-profile-fill` | TutorOcean profile work |
| `prompt-architect-refiner` | refining a prompt before it ships |
| `accommodations-email` | "accommodations email to X" (dictation: "accommodations scale"); pre-approved send, QP form attached, self-logging |

When a skill run exposes a gap, improve the skill before moving on. Runtime-specific catalog differences must be justified in `skills/PARITY.md`; undeclared differences are defects.

## Guardrails

- **95% rule.** Make no changes until you are 95% confident what Joshua wants. Ask until you are.
- **Lovable credits require confirmation.** Never take an action that spends or may spend Lovable credits without Joshua's explicit confirmation immediately before the action. Do not infer approval from a broader request to fix, build, deploy, or continue. Free Lovable controls and direct deployment paths may be used without this credit-spend confirmation.
- **Lovable Cloud deploys through Lovable.** LSAT U currently uses a Lovable Cloud-managed backend. Deploy its Edge Functions through the Lovable agent, not the Supabase CLI, unless the backend has first been migrated to a separately managed Supabase project. Confirm this route with Joshua before starting a Lovable deployment.
- **Root stays thin.** Don't accumulate files at the root; work products go to their homes (wiki, students, corpus, projects). Anything left at root gets filed before the session ends.
- **Delegate bulk work.** Exploration or analysis spanning 3+ files goes to a sub-agent that returns summaries, not file dumps.
- **Shell ergonomics.** Run one command per call — chained `cmd; cmd` and `rm -rf X && clone Y` forms trip permission denials that the split commands would not. Never poll with `sleep`. Use backgrounded execution for anything slow, and raise the timeout explicitly for known-slow ops (large moves, typecheck-gated pushes, full verify runs) rather than eating the default.
- **Search before creating.** One authoritative page per subject — update it, don't grow a parallel truth. `wiki/index.md` catalogs every page and updates on every ingest.
- **Preserve provenance and source hashes.** Never move, delete, or modify a migration source root — copy and hash-verify. Never follow a source symlink outside its registered root.
- **Conflicts stay explicit until adjudicated** — never silently overwrite one source generation with another.
- **Automation writes only generated files or fenced generated sections,** through compare-and-swap.
- **Obey nested instruction files** — they win over broader ones for their subtree. LSATU's recovered `CLAUDE.md`/`AGENTS.md` are inviolate: never regenerate them without Joshua's explicit approval.
- **Memory = native automemory.** "Remember that…" writes one fact per file to the native store; `MEMORY.md` indexes it. This file is the rules; memory is learned facts — never mix them. Transcripts and raw captures never auto-promote.
- **Append meaningful system changes** to `wiki/log.md`; log locked decisions to `decisions/log.md`.

## Dual-runtime (Codex + Claude)

- Begin at the Git-root `AGENTS.md` and follow directory-scoped instruction files toward the working path; the nearest file wins for its subtree.
- Keep root instructions inside the runtime byte budget. Move narrow rules to the narrowest owning directory rather than growing this file.
- Activated skills are **copies** in `.agents/skills/` (from `skills/codex/`); agent definitions convert to `.codex/agents/*.toml` with the instructions in `developer_instructions`.
- Claude reads `CLAUDE.md` and never this file. Anything that must hold for both runtimes goes in BOTH files — when you change it here, mirror it there; keep the routing map and guardrails byte-identical between them.
- Activation is unequal by design: Codex reads copies in `.agents/skills/`, Claude reads copies in `.claude/skills/`. Codex and Claude both activate the LLM Wiki ingest, query, and lint workflows. Other runtime-specific activation differences remain declared in `skills/PARITY.md`. Never have separate runtimes writing the same page concurrently.
- Never have Claude and Codex editing the same file at once; pass work between them by session handoff (summarize → clear → paste). At ~250k tokens, hand off rather than riding autocompact.

## developer

<multi_agent_role>You are an agent in a team of agents collaborating to complete a task.

You can spawn sub-agents to handle subtasks, and those sub-agents can spawn their own sub-agents. All agents in the team, including the agents that you can assign tasks to, are equally intelligent and capable, and have access to the same set of tools.

You can use `spawn_agent` to create a new agent, `followup_task` to give an existing agent a new task and trigger a turn, and `send_message` to pass a message to a running agent.
`send_message` calls may be read by a human, so ensure they are legible. Always put proper spaces between words and/or numbers.
Child agents can also spawn their own sub-agents.

When you provide a response in the final channel, that content is immediately delivered back to your parent agent.
In addition, your final answer may be read by a human, so ensure it is legible.

You will receive messages in the analysis channel in the form:
```
Message Type: NEW_TASK | MESSAGE | FINAL_ANSWER
Task name: <recipient>
Sender: <author>
Payload:
<payload text>
```
You may also see them addressed as to=/root/..., which indicates your identity is /root/...

Note that collaboration tools cannot be called from inside `functions.exec`. Call `spawn_agent`, `send_message`, `followup_task`, `wait_agent`, `interrupt_agent`, and `list_agents` only as direct tool calls using the recipient shown in their tool definitions, such as `to=functions.collaboration.spawn_agent`, since they are intentionally absent from the `functions.exec` `tools.*` namespace. Available tools in `functions.exec` are explicitly described with a `tools` namespace in the developer message.

All agents share the same directory. In detail:
- All agents have access to the same container and filesystem as you.
- All agents use the same current working directory.
- As a result, edits made by one agent are immediately visible to all other agents.

When calling `wait_agent`, prefer longer waits (minutes) to avoid busy polling.

There are 4 available concurrency slots, meaning that up to 4 agents can be active at once, including you.

Full-history forks (`fork_turns` omitted or `"all"`) inherit the parent model and reasoning effort and do not accept overrides. Only set `model` or `reasoning_effort` when explicitly requested by the user, applicable `AGENTS.md` instructions, or skill instructions; when doing so, set `fork_turns` to `"none"` or a positive integer string.</multi_agent_role>

2026-07-17\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Corpus — LSAT teaching knowledge\\r\\n\\r\\nThe second knowledge system: Joshua\'s LSAT teaching knowledge, built incrementally on the Karpathy LLM-wiki pattern as materials are ingested (books + notes, LSAT Lab, 7Sage material, drills, explanations). Eventually integrates into LSAT U as platform feedstock.\\r\\n\\r\\nFills through supervised ingests, one source at a time, logged in [[../wiki/log|the wiki log]]. Raw materials land in `raw/` first (immutable); the corpus holds the synthesized, interlinked teaching pages.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Conventions\\r\\n\\r\\n- Pages are LLM-written and LLM-maintained; Joshua curates sources and directs emphasis.\\r\\n- Wikilink corpus concepts into `students/<name>/` pages when a student is working that area, and into LSAT U material as it ships.\\r\\n- Question-type taxonomy, method pages, and drill-design principles get their own pages as they accumulate — no empty scaffolding in advance.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Pages\\r\\n\\r\\n### `lectures/` · read-aloud scripts and teaching visuals\\r\\n\\r\\n- [[lectures/index]] · LSAT Lab source transcripts, teleprompter scripts, initial concept boards, and worked-question visuals. Packages preserve source timestamps and explicit completeness checks.\\r\\n\\r\\n### `Curriculum/` — the LR curriculum (ingested 2026-07-17)\\r\\n\\r\\n118 markdown pages recovered from `raw/sources/lsat-curriculum/_stragglers/local-ai-gather/…/Curriculum/` — the corpus folder tree had been scaffolded from this source in the 2026-07-16 rebuild, but the files themselves were never copied. Each topic carries `Notes/` (the teaching) and `Homework/` (the assigned drills).\\r\\n\\r\\n| Topic | Pages | Topic | Pages |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Main Conclusion | 21 | Necessary Assumption | 7 |\\r\\n| Flaw and Famous Flaws | 11 | Conditional Logic | 7 |\\r\\n| Abstract | 6 | Causation | 6 |\\r\\n| Principle | 4 | Role | 4 |\\r\\n| Sufficient Assumption | 4 | Must be True | 3 |\\r\\n| Strengthen | 2 | Parallel | 2 |\\r\\n| Rebuttals | 2 | Comparison | 1 |\\r\\n| Method | 1 | Most Supported | 1 |\\r\\n| Parallel Flaw | 1 | | |\\r\\n\\r\\n- `Curriculum/_question-feature-index/` — 33 pages indexing question features across PTs 101–159 (famous-flaws, complex-conditionals, 2-speaker, science, except, …).\\r\\n- `Curriculum/_meta/lr-taxonomy.md` — the LR question-type taxonomy.\\r\\n- `Curriculum/LR/` — 2 homework pages exported live from Google Drive (pilot, 2026-07-17).\\r\\n\\r\\n**Known gaps:** topics scaffolded but still unfilled — Agree Disagree, Bootcamp, Match Family, Must Be False, Paradox, Quantifiers, Weaken, Reading Comprehension. Their source material is split between `raw/sources/lsat-curriculum/LSAT DUMP/` (many entries are stale `.gdoc` Drive pointers whose IDs no longer resolve) and Google Drive itself, where a past migration converted docs to PDFs/scans under new IDs. Recovering those needs a Drive-side pass, not a local copy.\\r\\n\\r\\n### `lr-explanations/` — models for writing LR explanations (2026-09-26)\\r\\n\\r\\n- [[lr-explanations/golden-set]] — the 51 LSAT Lab explanations new LSAT U explanations are modelled on, with the quality rubric.\\r\\n- [[lr-explanations/models/index]] — model library: 334 top-tier existing explanations, 10 to 20 per LR type, with what the best ones do for each type.\\r\\n- [[lr-explanations/drafts/pilot-2026-09-27]] — 20-question pilot of new explanations, waiting for Joshua.\\r\\n- [[lr-explanations/golden-set-observations]] — strong-vs-loose comparison, step structure per type, voice notes.\\r\\n- [[lr-explanations/golden-set-candidates]] — all 80 candidates and the type x feature coverage grid.\\r\\n- [[lr-explanations/fixes/index]] — the fix list: one line per issue, with detail files beside it (revamp list, Choice Note issues).\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

Obsidian Web Clipper captures of new.theloophole.com, 2026-08-24 onward.\\n**Read, never modify.** The synthesised wiki built from these lives at `corpus/loophole-online/`.\\n\\n## Filing rule\\n\\nFolders mirror the platform\'s own URL structure, so every clip files itself from its\\n`source:` frontmatter. No judgment call required.\\n\\n| `source:` contains | Goes in |\\n|---|---|\\n| `/stage/translation/` | `training-camp/translation/` |\\n| `/stage/learn/` | `training-camp/learn/` |\\n| `/stage/accuracy/` | `training-camp/accuracy/` |\\n| `/concept-review/` | `training-camp/concept-review/` |\\n| `/workouts-and-routines/workouts/` | `workouts/` |\\n| `/workouts-and-routines/routines/` | `routines/` |\\n\\n`_duplicates/` holds clips whose source URL already appears in a primary folder.\\n`_defective/` holds clips that captured nothing.\\n\\n## New clips\\n\\nThe Web Clipper writes to `Clippings/` root. Move new Loophole captures here and sort\\nthem by the table above, then ingest into `corpus/loophole-online/` and update its\\n`SOURCES.md` ledger. Do not start a second wiki folder.\\n\\n## Current contents (2026-08-30)\\n\\n| Folder | Clips |\\n|---|---|\\n| `training-camp/translation/` | 25 |\\n| `training-camp/learn/` | 9 |\\n| `training-camp/accuracy/` | 1 |\\n| `training-camp/concept-review/` | 2 |\\n| `workouts/` | 8 |\\n| `routines/` | 1 |\\n| `_duplicates/` | 5 |\\n| `_defective/` | 1 |\\n| **Total** | **52** (46 unique pages) |\\n\\n## Two things not in here, on purpose\\n\\n- **`Why Theres No Such Thing as Just a Detail • LO.md`** is still in `Clippings/` root.\\n  Despite the filename it is not a Loophole clip: it holds a Postgres migration for a\\n  `practice_test_runs` table (LSAT U work) with no frontmatter. Filing it here would\\n  bury it. It needs rescuing to the LSAT U repo or deleting, and the real page needs\\n  re-clipping.\\n- **Duplicates were not deleted**, only separated. Nothing in this tree is ever removed\\n  without asking.\\n\\r\\nFILE: C:\\\\Users\\\\Joshu\\\\Documents\\\\Ai-os\\\\Clippings\\\\loophole-online\\\\routines\\\\Translation Foundation Routine • LO.md\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"Translation Foundation Routine • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/routines/translation-foundation-routine\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## Quick Facts\\n\\n### Will This Help Me?\\n\\nThis will help you if\\n\\nYou\'re just starting out in your Translation skill building.\\n\\nSkip for now if\\n\\nDon\'t skip this Routine.\\n\\nchallenge indexPretty Chill\\n\\nlevelBeginner\\n\\nfrequencyDaily Foundation\\n\\n![calendar](https://new.theloophole.com/_next/static/media/calendar.778ddbbe.svg)\\n\\nstudy block length2-4 hours\\n\\n## Schedule (4 workouts)\\n\\nEnsure you complete the workouts **in order** to benefit the most from this routine.\\n\\n1\\n\\n### Translation + CLIR Drill\\n\\n≥ 3 sections\\n\\n2\\n\\n### Extracurricular Reading\\n\\n#### Instructions\\n\\n1. Every day, translate elevated reading material from outside the LSAT, using the following sources:\\n\\t1. **[ScienceDaily.com](https://www.sciencedaily.com/)**\\n\\t\\t1. Ideal daily reading for those who feel alienated by scientific language or fear science passages in RC\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t2. Be sure to choose the articles that challenge you the most, not articles that look familiar or interesting\\n\\t\\t2. **[Aeon.co](https://aeon.co/)**\\n\\t\\t1. The most similar articles to LSAT RC on the internet\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t2. Reading an Aeon.co article every day is a great habit if you have no specific topic-based weaknesses, but would like to make RC-style reading a habit.\\n\\t\\t3. **[ALDaily.com](https://www.aldaily.com/)**\\n\\t\\t1. Ideal daily reading for those who feel alienated by abstract language, art, humanities, and pretension.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t2. Be sure to choose the articles that look the most unfamiliar, not articles that speak to your pre-existing interests.\\n\\t\\t4. **[NYT Guide to Essential Knowledge](https://www.amazon.com/York-Times-Guide-Essential-Knowledge-ebook/dp/B005EXS7E4/ref=tmm_kin_swatch_0)**\\n\\t\\t1. The single best way to fix gaps in common knowledge\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t2. Here is a list of sections in the Guide that are relevant to LSAT. If any of these topics would trouble you as an RC passage, read over that section of the guide.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t3. **LSAT Relevant Guide Sections**: Architecture, Zoology, Botany, History, Art, Photography, Literature, Music (the serious parts about jazz and blues, especially), Film, Dance, Drama, Climate & Weather and Glossary of Weather Words (In Geography), Government, Law, Philosophy, Biology, Chemistry, Physics, Astronomy, Geology\\n\\t\\t5. **[Crash Course](https://www.youtube.com/@crashcourse/playlists)**\\n\\t\\t1. Not a written source, but Crash Course is too useful for solving common knowledge gaps to not include\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t2. Watch Crash Course while you\'re in line, waiting for someone, on hold, brushing your teeth, etc. Any time you could be scrolling on your phone, watch Crash Course instead.\\n\\t\\t\\t\\t3. **Top Five LSAT Relevant Crash Course Sections**: Intellectual Property (Ellen\'s favorite for LSAT students), Economics, US Government & Politics, Art History, and Philosophy\\n\\n3\\n\\n### Vocab Training\\n\\n### Once A Week, Do This Instead\\n\\n1\\n\\n### Translation Marathon\\n\\n8 sections\\n\\n≥ 1x per week\\n\\n## Ellen\'s Advice\\n\\n### Why Are We Doing This?\\n\\nThe Translation + CLIR Drills in this Routine are the foundation of your entire LSAT prep journey. This is the most important homework you will do during your LSAT prep training, even compared to PTs.\\n\\nThis Routine builds a foundation in LR Translation before we port your Translation skills over to RC in the next Routine.\\n\\n### How This Should Be Done\\n\\nYour progression toward the Translation time and accuracy goals is primarily determined by two things: **volume** and **following the instructions.**\\n\\nTo maximize your progress in this Routine, do all your Translation + CLIR Drills consecutively; do not take breaks between Translation + CLIR Drills. Do not review your Translation + CLIR Drills in between sections. All review should be done in a separate sitting.\\n\\n**Do at least 3 consecutive Translation + CLIR Drills per day, if possible.** Ideally, you would complete 6 Translation + CLIR drills consecutively per day. Doing 1 Translation + CLIR Drill per day will not improve your skills; it will keep your skills the same. You will only start improving on your second consecutive Translation + CLIR Drill; this is why it is so useful to do *at least* 3 consecutive Translation + CLIR Drills every day.\\n\\nAdd a Translation Marathon to your Daily Practice at least once per week. If you can manage to do Translation + CLIR Marathons more often, you will progress to the time goal faster.\\n\\nExtracurricular Reading and Vocab should always be done at the end of your Routine to gradually expand your common sense in the subject areas where you\'re weakest. If you struggle with science passages, emphasize daily science engagement in the Extracurricular Reading. If you struggle with abstract language, emphasize daily humanities engagement in the Extracurricular Reading.\\n\\n### How to Move On\\n\\n**Once you are halfway to your Translation + CLIR Drill time goal, move to the next Routine: Translation + Decaf.**\\n\\n**The time goal for Translation + CLIR Drills is 20 minutes.** The accommodated time goals for Translation + CLIR Drills are 25 minutes for 53-minute accommodations and 30 minutes for 70-minute accommodations. Meeting this Translation + CLIR Drill time goal is only valid under two conditions: you have a CLIR for every stimulus and you have translated and quipped every stimulus in the section out loud. Ideally, you should hit -5 accuracy against the CLIR Answer Key consistently in order to beat the time goal, but this accuracy goal may be adjusted according to your score goals.\\n\\n**Example of When to Move On**: You started at 52 minutes per out loud Translation + CLIR Drill section and you don\'t have accommodations, so your time goal is 20 minutes. The midpoint of 52 and 20 is 36, so you would move on when your time hits 36 minutes.\\r\\nFILE: C:\\\\Users\\\\Joshu\\\\Documents\\\\Ai-os\\\\Clippings\\\\loophole-online\\\\training-camp\\\\accuracy\\\\How to Camo • LO.md\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"How to Camo • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/accuracy/video/how-to-camo\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## How to Camo\\n\\n0:14\\n\\n/\\n\\n8:25\\n\\n- 0:00How to Camo so I comment on Camo in other videos But I just want to have one dedicated video in case anyone\'s bopping around and they were just interested to learn about Camo solo independent of any other topic\\n- 0:11and so Camo what is it? it is a way to review your sections in which you put in all the answers that you chose in a section and Camo will generate a list of questions for you to return to that includes all of your wrong answers and an unknown number of decoy correct answers\\n- 0:26and so You then have this anonymized list you go back through all of the questions you redo them earnestly And then you place your new answers in Camo you tell it what you pick this time And then it sorts your mistakes into conceptual gaps those are the questions you missed twice you missed both\\n- 0:40in the original take of the Section and in your Camo pass miss reads which are the questions you missed in your original take and you got right in your Camo pass Self-doubt\\n- 0:48which are questions you got right and were decoys and then they they come back wrong in the Camo Changed away from right answer and self-confidence Those are the ones that you got right both times a decoy that comes up that actually you stay with your answer Which is great\\n- 1:02So though that is how you Camo it gives you a lot of really great information because of course Miss reads questions that you were able to correct those are really low-hanging fruit that we want to focus on in terms of like okay What exactly did I do in the moment? Just when I was doing this section\\n- 1:15originally that I can now change that Enabled me to get it right in camera you have a lot more clues to go on because you actually did get it right in Camo so like what did you do differently?\\n- 1:24And then you have your conceptual gaps These are the ones that you got wrong in Camo again either by choosing the same answer or choosing a different answer Those are our more sticky ones and ones that we\'ll need to review more fully Maybe put on The Island to come back to later But they also\\n- 1:38they give us a different sort of clue than the miss reads of okay These are the ones that I genuinely there\'s like something missing that I don\'t understand and we need to resolve it over time\\n- 1:46A self-doubt that\'s where you know you got it right and changed wrong these indicate an issue With second-guessing yourself obviously self-doubt that\'s the name of it\\n- 1:55And it means that you need to work on using your gut instinct and not questioning yourself going with your gut and not Staying around to undo your good thoughts\\n- 2:05and so those are the basics of just what Camo is and how you go about it now Let me give you the kind of finer points to enable you to Camo really really well because\\n- 2:12there are a few things that people Do with Camo that really mess them up And so first thing is you should wait at least 24 hours After doing a section and before Camoing it\\n- 2:23and why do I say this I say this because when people Camo right after they do A section their score is always worse\\n- 2:29Because they are essentially using Camo as a way to check the section and all they really want is the score\\n- 2:34They don\'t really care as much about actually doing well on the Camo they tend to rush it They tend to just choose the same answers that they chose originally under some kind of weird guise of like My integrity says that I chose this\\n- 2:46and I don\'t want to change you know and cheat or something That that weird thing that people do in camera sometimes It\'s not exclusive to doing the section right after you did it originally,\\n- 2:56but you\'re allowed to change your answer in Camo It\'s not like dishonest to have a new idea And so don\'t just stick with your same answers out of some weird misguided sense of loyalty.\\n- 3:04That\'s not like a real thing And so you should wait at least 24 hours Simply because it will improve your Camo score because you\'re more likely to genuinely redo the questions\\n- 3:14You\'ll have a much more level head when you go about them And of course instead of waiting 24 hours You could also have a Camo day once or twice a week where you extend out greater than 24 hours before you Camo which the The reason for it just kind of compounds of like the longer you are away from\\n- 3:30it the more level-headed the less emotional and the more Insights you will be able to glean from your sections, especially doing them in a bigger batch\\n- 3:37Another Camo pro tip do not do Camo Before you prep for the day before you do all your sections do your Camo as the last thing you do in your prep when I build routines for people of what they\'re gonna do every day\\n- 3:48I always put Camo at the end and they always move it to first because they just want to check their sections and get the scores\\n- 3:53But the reason why I always put it at the end is because I don\'t want you being Reactive to your Camo results and immediately being like oh, I just have a problem with Method\\n- 4:02Okay, now I need to make sure I get all the Methods This is one of those things that essentially no one wants to do.\\n- 0:06Nobody wants to quip the answer choices, but the quipping of the answer choices to me is actually the highlight slash most important moment in the doing of any question,\\n- 0:18because the quipping of the answer choices is when you get the chance to figure out whether that answer is right with actual, you know, real data as opposed to just kind of skipping to the end.\\n- 0:30And so let me explain. Let me explain. I want people to quip the answers in a certain way.\\n- 0:35Obviously, you read the answer, you translate the answer, you get to that quipping stage. In that quipping stage, ideally, you\'re going to quip that answer through melding together the information from the stimulus with the answer choice\\n- 0:48and having some sort of commentary that\'s very much informed by the stimulus. You\'re bringing to bear a lot of the concepts, even the phrases that are included in the\\n- 0:58you bring them down into the answer choices for that little roundtable discussion that just involves you and no one else at a roundtable.\\n- 1:04And in that moment, you are conceptually engaging with the answer choice according to what the question is really asking,\\n- 1:12but using evidence from the stimulus to have that interesting discussion. And so when you\'re having that interesting discussion, you\'re actually forming the basis of evidence for a judgment.\\n- 1:23And it\'s really important. As we would kind of know from just the LSAT and like circular reasoning and things, it\'s really important to have evidence for a judgment or a claim or a Conclusion before you go to that judgment, claim or Conclusion.\\n- 1:39And this presumption, which I feel like we can all agree on, right? You would rather start with evidence and then move toward a claim rather than starting with a claim and moving toward evidence.\\n- 1:50You\'re just more likely to get a valid claim or Conclusion out of that sort of arrangement, right? When you start with evidence rather than starting with the claim.\\n- 1:59However, when most people engage with LSAT answer choices, they do not want to start with evidence. In fact, they want to read it very read the answer choice very quickly, silently, and they want to immediately have a right wrong judgment.\\n- 2:12And that right wrong judgment has to be, I guess, informed by like magic or intuition or liking or disliking or all kinds of things that are not actual metrics or actual evidence.\\n- 2:24They will see when people do demos and things like that on video, they\'ll be like, oh, you have to know the fate of the answer immediately.\\n- 2:31You can\'t have any sort of processing of the answer. You have to just read it and immediately and intuitively know if it\'s right or wrong.\\n- 2:37And while that is a wonderful goal and perhaps it is something that could happen on some answer choices in the future.\\n- 2:43And it\'s something like, I know what that feels like. It\'s not like I\'m saying it doesn\'t exist. If you were right now trying to remedy a big score gap, doing an impression of somebody who\'s at like expert level, who made a video and instantly and intuitively saw why an answer is right or wrong, doing\\n- 3:00an impression of them where you read an answer and you\'re like, yeah, so it\'s wrong. That\'s not going to get you anywhere\\n- 3:06because you\'re right there forcing knowledge that you don\'t actually have. You\'re forcing a judgment that you don\'t actually really have data for. The\\n- 3:15people feel so much pressure to make that judgment really quickly that they skip the information analysis stage. And that\'s what really the quipping of the answer choices is.\\n- 3:26It\'s that information analysis stage in which like this is the meat of doing the question, right? I mean, this is when you take the answer choice and you\'re like, hey, does this actually function\\n- 3:36according to the question stem in regards to the stimulus? And in a way, what I\'m asking there of like the level of engagement I\'m asking for, it is asking, is this right?\\n- 3:45But it\'s not asking, is this right? By literally saying the words, is this right? Because the words, is this right? Is like way too referential, way too generalized.\\n- 3:54It\'s going to lead to very kind of cheap answer choices of like, I don\'t like this. This one\'s good. This one could be it.\\n- 4:00Notice that there\'s like no content to those answers. They\'re like mass market generalized quips. Like I talked about in my last video where you could just pick them up and put them on a different stimulus or not a different stimulus, a different answer choice. And they would make\\n- 4:15just as much sense. You want to stay away from that sort of quipping because it means you aren\'t really engaging with what you read and you don\'t have real evidence that\'s informed by specificity.\\n- 4:24And you might be saying to yourself like, oh, you know, how do I have time for this? I\'m going to go over that in another video. So don\'t freak out about that quite yet.\\n- 4:30But I will tell you that when you see my question, my question stem demos and my question doing demos, you\'ll see me go through answer choices. And I did a speed run at one point. You can go watch the speed run if you want, where I went through and I actually tried to do a section\\n- 4:46out loud in 35 minutes. And the one thing I gave myself perMission to do was I wasn\'t going to translate the answers out loud because I was like, OK, doing that out loud in 35, you know, it\'s a lot slower to talk things out in general. And so I know I\'m not going to be able to do it if I translate\\n- 4:58the answers out loud. Some of them I still did just, you know, it was intuitive. It felt like the right thing to do in the moment. But one thing you\'ll notice is I\'m still clipping those answers out loud.\\n- 5:06That didn\'t go anywhere because that is the fundamentally important action of every question is actually talking through the evidence for and against a given answer choices correctness in regard to an exploration and investigation through curiosity of like, how does this engage with the question\\n- 5:24through the lens of the stimulus? Like that\'s what I want to find out. So when you see me in those videos, even in the speed run, but then, of course, in the longer question type explanations as well, you\'ll see me engage with every answer in this way. And so I really want you to pay close attention\\n- 5:39when you watch my demos of how I\'m engaging with those answer choices. I\'m actually not just doing a big explanation or something.\\n- 5:46I\'m telling you how I\'m thinking about the answer right after I translated it. And that way I\'m thinking about the answer is what then leads me to my verdict on that answer choice.\\n- 5:55And so if you remember one thing from this video, don\'t do circular reasoning where you decide the Conclusion and then you just, you know, try and a priori justify it using the fact that it\'s right to show that it\'s right or wrong to show that it\'s wrong. So\\n- 6:09that isn\'t going to get you to high accuracy on this test. Instead, translate the answer, equip the answer in regards to the stimulus, bringing information from the stimulus down into context, have a conversation with the answer through the lens of the question type.\\n- 6:22I think I\'ve said the stimulus and the question type are both the lens. You can do it either way.\\n- 6:27And going from there to determine what is right and what is wrong. All right. I hope that this helped. I hope it provided a little more specificity on exactly how clipping in the answers works and why it\'s so valuable. Thank\\n- 6:37you.\\n\\n##### Day 2\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 1.0 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"4a7e55","wall_time_seconds":0.3069874,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":212,"output":"raw/sources\\\\lsat-u\\\\clippings\\\\Cluster Trees A Visual Translation.md\\nraw/sources\\\\conditional-gmail-2026-10-02\\\\19cac9fef4878085_Assumption_Objection answer key.pdf\\nraw/sources\\\\conditional-gmail-2026-10-02\\\\19cac9e21b2cebb3_Assumption_Objection answer key.pdf\\nraw/sources\\\\conditional-gmail-2026-10-02\\\\199c0c8a74c3486d_Homework_ Keyword Extraction and Translation of Question Stems.pdf\\ncorpus\\\\Curriculum\\\\Abstract\\\\Homework\\\\Quiz - Translation and Cluster Sentences.md\\ncorpus\\\\Curriculum\\\\LR\\\\Assumption and Objection Family\\\\Strengthen\\\\Homework\\\\Principle + Strengthen HWK.md\\ncorpus\\\\Curriculum\\\\Flaw and Famous Flaws\\\\Homework\\\\Pop Quiz - Assumption vs Objection.md\\nraw/sources\\\\lsat-curriculum\\\\_stragglers\\\\recovered-ai-gather\\\\Claude 2\\\\02_PROJECTS\\\\Curriculum\\\\Curriculum\\\\Flaw and Famous Flaws\\\\Notes\\\\Capturing Assumptions and the Tricks of the Car Sales Man_dup1.pdf\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"6ed0f3","wall_time_seconds":0.280024,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2366,"output":"# Chapter 5 — Assumptions & The Objection\\n\\n*Transcribed from the quiz screenshots in [[Untitled 3.md]]. \\"Loophole\\" is rendered throughout as \\"Objection\\"; everything else is verbatim. Questions 1, 2, and 6 were not captured.*\\n\\n## Question 3\\n\\n*Hoodoo and Keith are kitten best friends who routinely engage in mischievous conduct spanning everything from play-wrestling to napping on radiators. As a pair, they have already gnawed through half a cigar, gotten trapped under an area rug, and claimed the counter as their own. Therefore, Hoodoo and Keith knocked over a vase of daffodils.*\\n\\nWhich one of the following is not an Objection in the argument above?\\n\\n- A. What if kitten best friends aren\'t strong enough to knock over a vase?\\n- **B. What if knocking over a vase of daffodils doesn\'t qualify as mischievous conduct?** ✅\\n- C. What if Hoodoo and Keith have never had access to a vase of daffodils?\\n\\n## Question 4\\n\\nHow do you design an Objection?\\n\\n- A. What if the conclusion is not true?\\n- B. What if one of the premises is not really true?\\n- **C. What if [really bad thing for the argument]?** ✅\\n\\n## Question 5\\n\\nHow will you know if your Objection works?\\n\\n- **A. If the Objection is possible, the conclusion is no longer proven.** ✅\\n- B. The Objection works as a valid prephrase.\\n- C. The Objection addresses key concepts in the argument.\\n\\n## Question 7\\n\\n*Keith is afraid of new people and gets stressed in new environments. He is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house for the first time, so he will hide all weekend.*\\n\\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\\n\\n- A. What if Keith won\'t hide?\\n- **B. What if Keith doesn\'t hide when he\'s stressed?** ✅\\n- C. What if Keith\'s aunt cancels their weekend plans?\\n\\n## Question 8 — explanation\\n\\n*Keith is afraid of new people and gets stressed in new environments. He is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house for the first time, so he will hide all weekend.*\\n\\n*What if Keith\'s aunt cancels their weekend plans?*\\nThe premises told us that Keith is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house. We can\'t negate the premises.\\n\\n*What if Keith won\'t hide?*\\nThe conclusion claims that Keith will hide. We can\'t just negate the conclusion and call it an Objection.\\n\\n## Question 9\\n\\n*Hoodoo and Keith love to watch reality TV and paw at the speaker when people speak. This proves that all felines do in fact respond to language, since Hoodoo and Keith are house cats.*\\n\\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\\n\\n- A. What if Hoodoo and Keith don\'t really understand language but instead are simply responding to the sounds of reality TV?\\n- B. What if Hoodoo and Keith are becoming more like wild cats?\\n- **C. What if Hoodoo and Keith are not representative of all felines?** ✅\\n\\n## Question 10 — explanation\\n\\n*Hoodoo and Keith love to watch reality TV and paw at the speaker when people speak. This proves that all felines do in fact respond to language, since Hoodoo and Keith are house cats.*\\n\\n*What if Hoodoo and Keith don\'t really understand language but instead are simply responding to the sounds of reality TV?*\\nThe argument only assumes that Hoodoo and Keith \\"respond\\" to language, not that they understand language. Pointing out that Hoodoo and Keith may not understand language does not affect the validity of the argument.\\n\\n*What if Hoodoo and Keith are becoming more like wild cats?*\\nHoodoo and Keith becoming more like wild cats does not affect whether or not we can generalize from their behaviors to those of all felines.\\n\\r\\n# Objection — Mini-Quiz\\n\\n5 questions. Pick one answer each. Answer key at the bottom.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### 1\\n\\n*Hoodoo and Keith are kitten best friends who routinely engage in mischievous conduct spanning everything from play-wrestling to napping on radiators. As a pair, they have already gnawed through half a cigar, gotten trapped under an area rug, and claimed the counter as their own. Therefore, Hoodoo and Keith knocked over a vase of daffodils.*\\n\\nWhich one of the following is **not** an Objection in the argument above?\\n\\n- A. What if kitten best friends aren\'t strong enough to knock over a vase?\\n- B. What if knocking over a vase of daffodils doesn\'t qualify as mischievous conduct?\\n- C. What if Hoodoo and Keith have never had access to a vase of daffodils?\\n\\n---\\n\\n### 2\\n\\nHow do you design an Objection?\\n\\n- A. What if the conclusion is not true?\\n- B. What if one of the premises is not really true?\\n- C. What if [really bad thing for the argument]?\\n\\n---\\n\\n### 3\\n\\nHow will you know if your Objection works?\\n\\n- A. If the Objection is possible, the conclusion is no longer proven.\\n- B. The Objection works as a valid prephrase.\\n- C. The Objection addresses key concepts in the argument.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### 4\\n\\n*Keith is afraid of new people and gets stressed in new environments. He is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house for the first time, so he will hide all weekend.*\\n\\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\\n\\n- A. What if Keith won\'t hide?\\n- B. What if Keith doesn\'t hide when he\'s stressed?\\n- C. What if Keith\'s aunt cancels their weekend plans?\\n\\n---\\n\\n### 5\\n\\n*Hoodoo and Keith love to watch reality TV and paw at the speaker when people speak. This proves that all felines do in fact respond to language, since Hoodoo and Keith are house cats.*\\n\\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\\n\\n- A. What if Hoodoo and Keith don\'t really understand language but instead are simply responding to the sounds of reality TV?\\n- B. What if Hoodoo and Keith are becoming more like wild cats?\\n- C. What if Hoodoo and Keith are not representative of all felines?\\n\\n---\\n\\n## Answer key\\n\\n**1. B** — the other two attack the argument; this one doesn\'t.\\n\\n**2. C** — you don\'t negate the conclusion or the premises. You ask what would wreck the argument.\\n\\n**3. A** — if the Objection is possible and the conclusion no longer follows, it works.\\n\\n**4. B** — A negates the conclusion, C negates a premise. B breaks the link between stress and hiding.\\n\\n**5. C** — A: the argument only claims they respond, not that they understand. B: turning into wild cats doesn\'t touch whether you can generalize. C: kills the generalization from two house cats to all felines.\\n\\r\\n![[Pasted image 20260830033305.png]]\\n\\n![[Pasted image 20260830033457.png]]\\n\\n\\n![[Pasted image 20260830033508.png]]\\n\\n![[Pasted image 20260830033531.png]]\\n\\n![[Pasted image 20260830033545.png]]\\n\\n![[Pasted image 20260830033556.png]]\\n\\n![[Pasted image 20260830033605.png]]\\n\\n\\n![[Pasted image 20260830033705.png]]\\r\\n## Question 2\\n\\n_Alexander the Great ate ice only if it was flavored with honey or nectar, the latter mostly coming from agave plants. Without laborious harvesting, agave nectar cannot be used as flavoring._\\n\\nWhich one of the following is a valid diagram of the statements above?\\n\\nChapter 4Conditional Reasoning\\n\\n1 / 5 Complete\\n\\n## Question 2\\n\\n_Alexander the Great ate ice only if it was flavored with honey or nectar, the latter mostly coming from agave plants. Without laborious harvesting, agave nectar cannot be used as flavoring._\\n\\nWhich one of the following is a valid diagram of the statements above?\\r\\ncreate table if not exists public.practice_test_runs (\\n  id               text primary key,\\n  user_id          uuid not null default auth.uid() references auth.users (id) on delete cascade,\\n  pt               int not null,\\n  pace             text not null,\\n  legs             jsonb not null,\\n  current_leg      int not null default 0,\\n  break_after_leg  int not null,\\n  break_taken      boolean not null default false,\\n  break_started_at timestamptz,\\n  status           text not null check (status in (\'in-progress\',\'complete\',\'abandoned\')),\\n  revision         int not null default 0,\\n  completed_at     timestamptz,\\n  raw_correct      int,\\n  raw_total        int,\\n  scaled_score     int,\\n  score_source     text check (score_source in (\'official-table\',\'estimate\')),\\n  created_at       timestamptz not null default now(),\\n  updated_at       timestamptz not null default now(),\\n  unique (id, user_id)\\n);\\ncreate index if not exists practice_test_runs_user_status on public.practice_test_runs (user_id, status);\\nalter table public.practice_test_runs enable row level security;\\n\\ncreate or replace function public.touch_practice_test_runs()\\nreturns trigger language plpgsql set search_path = public as $$\\nbegin new.updated_at = now(); return new; end; $$;\\n\\ndrop trigger if exists practice_test_runs_touch on public.practice_test_runs;\\ncreate trigger practice_test_runs_touch before update on public.practice_test_runs\\n  for each row execute function public.touch_practice_test_runs();\\n\\ndrop policy if exists \\"pt_runs_owner_select\\" on public.practice_test_runs;\\ncreate policy \\"pt_runs_owner_select\\" on public.practice_test_runs for select using (user_id = auth.uid());\\ndrop policy if exists \\"pt_runs_owner_insert\\" on public.practice_test_runs;\\ncreate policy \\"pt_runs_owner_insert\\" on public.practice_test_runs for insert with check (user_id = auth.uid());\\ndrop policy if exists \\"pt_runs_owner_update\\" on public.practice_test_runs;\\ncreate policy \\"pt_runs_owner_update\\" on public.practice_test_runs for update using (user_id = auth.uid()) with check (user_id = auth.uid());\\ndrop policy if exists \\"admins_read_practice_test_runs\\" on public.practice_test_runs;\\ncreate policy \\"admins_read_practice_test_runs\\" on public.practice_test_runs for select to authenticated using (public.has_role(auth.uid(), \'admin\'));\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.5 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Warning: truncated output (original token count: 13865)\nTotal output lines: 1\n\n{"chunk_id":"7f0489","wall_time_seconds":0.3057236,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":13628,"output":"FILE: PT 111 S 3 Q 14 Translation Plus CLIR • LO.md\\r\\nFILE: How and Why to Do Decaf RC Translation • LO 1.md\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"How and Why to Do Decaf RC Translation • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/concept-review/rc-translation-strategy/how-and-why-to-do-decaf-rc-translation\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\n\\n***Decaf RC Translation Drill***\\n\\n**NOTE**: If you want to do your recaps out loud in the beginning to keep yourself honest about the quality and completeness of the recaps, that\'s totally fine. Transition to silent recaps when you feel like you\'re ready.\\n\\n1. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 1. Do it just like LR.\\n\\t1. This means all the details are included just like they would be on LR.\\n\\t\\t2. Quip after *every* Translation.\\n\\t\\t3. This standard applies to every paragraph you ever translate on RC.\\n2. Look away from paragraph 1 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 1 silently.\\n\\t1. The recap is not a summary, gist, or main point.\\n\\t\\t2. The recap should not omit names, dates, and lists.\\n\\t\\t3. The recap should not be a shopping list of information. It should be a story in your own words.\\n\\t\\t4. It is encouraged to mix up the order of the sentences and ideas.\\n\\t\\t5. This standard applies to every paragraph (or collection of paragraphs) you ever recap on RC.\\n3. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 2. Do it just like LR.\\n4. Look away from paragraph 2 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 2 silently.\\n5. Without looking back, recap the Translations of paragraphs 1 + 2 back to back silently.\\n6. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 3. Do it just like LR.\\n7. Look away from paragraph 3 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 3 silently.\\n8. Without looking back, recap the Translations of paragraphs 1 + 2 + 3 back to back silently.\\n9. Repeat this pattern for all remaining paragraphs.\\r\\nFILE: How to Use AI to Transcribe Your Cli Rs in Translation Plus Cli Rs • LO 1.md\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"How to Use AI to Transcribe Your Cli Rs in Translation Plus Cli Rs • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/how-to-use-ai-to-transcribe-your-cli-rs-in-translation-plus-cli-rs?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## How to Use AI to Transcribe Your CLIRs in Translation + CLIRs\\n\\n4:14\\n\\n/\\n\\n4:14\\n\\n- 0:00Hey, also using AI to transcribe your CLIRs as you\'re doing your Translation, CLIR Drills is a huge time saver that enables you to check your CLIRs against the CLIR answer key way faster, way easier.\\n- 0:11And so I just want to recommend a few pieces of software to enable you to do this more seamlessly. The best AI voice transcription tool I have found for a desktop computer or a laptop is Whisper Flow.\\n- 0:23I gave you the link in the public text of this video, and I am not like making any money from referring you to these sources at all.\\n- 0:29This is just genuinely like what I have on my computer that I like to use, that I recommend to friends.\\n- 0:33And so like there\'s no weird thing going on here. Don\'t be suspicious of me. But yeah, I really like Whisper Flow personally.\\n- 0:41It honestly feels like magic when you use it. Essentially, you just set a hot key on your computer. For me, it\'s the control key.\\n- 0:46And whenever I hold down the control key and I talk, it really dictates what I am trying to say super, super well.\\n- 0:53And so I absolutely love it. And you just hold down the key and talk and then it transcribes it nicely for you.\\n- 0:59And honestly, you could just open like a Google Doc or a notion or Microsoft Word, if that\'s what you want, whatever.\\n- 1:05And after doing your Translation, CLIR Drills, you\'re talking everything out in the Translation. OK, great. And then when you\'re sitting there, as soon as you get to the CLIR and you want it to start transcribing, just hold down the hot key and then say you\'re CLIR.\\n- 1:18And if you want it to kind of like auto organize your notes for you, you could say the number of the question and then the CLIR and then you just let go of the hot key and it auto transcribes.\\n- 1:28Isn\'t that amazing? It\'s so cool. We\'re like living in the future here. And so as you go through, then you can, you know, press the button and be like 15. What if\\n- 1:36dilemma? Whatever. And you get 16. What if existential crisis? And you just keep on going. But then at the end of the section, you could highlight it if you\'re using Google Docs and say like Gemini, please organize this for me\\n- 1:50and just ask Gemini to reformat it, not change any of your text, obviously, but you know, insert line breaks and things like that.\\n- 1:56If whisper flow didn\'t do it automatically for you, which would then make it super easy to go and check against the CLIR answer.\\n- 2:00Of course, I\'m probably gilding the lily here asking for line breaks. Line breaks are really that important or really hard to put in yourself. But I would like it if show and I would make line breaks for me.\\n- 2:10And then I also recommended super whisper as well. Super whisper is basically the exact same thing as whisper flow, except you download the model locally to your computer.\\n- 2:19And so it doesn\'t have to be connected to the Internet at all. It\'s totally fully private. Your data of what you\'re saying never goes\\n- 2:24to any sort of server off of your computer, which that may be a priority for some people.\\n- 2:30The only reason that super whisper is not my personal favorite tool is that whisper flow, I think it just tends to be a little bit more accurate, a little bit more contextually aware, a little bit faster. But, you know, different people may have different priorities.\\n- 2:43Also, super whisper is a little bit cheaper. But whisper flow and super whisper both have free tiers where if you\'re only using this to do your CLIRs, you probably can stay in the free tier and you won\'t actually have to pay anything at all. But if you don\'t want to install extra\\n- 2:59software on your computer, you honestly could just use Google Docs and the dictation tool that\'s inside of Google Docs and then ask Gemini to be like, hey, you know, format this nicely for me.\\n- 3:08It\'s more just that the dictation isn\'t quite as high quality within Docs. But you can, of course, experiment with this yourself, see what workflow works best for you. But I don\'t want you to have to, you know, go and relisten to the whole recording in order to get your CLIRs.\\n- 3:22I mean, that would honestly really suck. I wouldn\'t want to do that. And I don\'t want you to have to take time out to type out your CLIRs just so you can easily check them against the CLIRance. Because again, that will take more time than is needed.\\n- 3:33One other option, honestly, if you wanted to get briefly like a subscription to loom loom with AI, well, you could just loom your entire Translation + CLIR Drill and it\'ll have both the Translation and the CLIR for you. But then it does a transcript, which you could then copy paste\\n- 3:49into Google Docs and be like, hey, Gemini, go organize this for me and put all my CLIRs in a list or something like that. And maybe you would say CLIR before you CLIR if you want to take that route of just the loom transcription rather than only transcribing your CLIRs alone.\\n- 4:02But all of these are just really nice little efficiency hacks that I like. I\'m a bit of an AI hobbyist. And so I hope that these are helpful to you as they\'ve been helpful to me. All right. I\'ll see you soon.\\n\\n##### Day 2\\n\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\n\\nThe Best AI Transcription I\'ve Found: [https://wisprflow.ai/](https://wisprflow.ai/)\\n\\nLocal Only AI Transcription: [https://superwhisper.com/](https://superwhisper.com/)\\n\\n...or literally just use Google Docs or Loom\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\nFILE: How Will This Ever Work in 35 Minutes • LO 1.md\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"How Will This Ever Work in 35 Minutes • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/how-will-this-ever-work-in-35-minutes?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## How Will This Ever Work in 35 Minutes?\\n\\n0:56\\n\\n/\\n\\n7:28\\n\\n- 0:00How could all this nonsense ever work in 35, right? How could it ever? Especially the quipping. That seems the most superfluous.\\n- 0:08But I will tell you it actually does work at 35 and I\'m gonna use myself as a little bit of an anecdotal example here, which is something I tend to not want to do because I don\'t I don\'t I don\'t like making my prep like part of the discussion.\\n- 0:21But you know, sometimes it\'s useful.
