Ah, where one\'s hatred of abstraction comes home to roost, right? People very often hate abstraction on the LSAT. They complain about it all the time.\\n- 0:10And Method, it\'s really the most abstract task you\'re going to be asked to do on the entire LSAT. Of course, very similar to Argument Part, but since you have to do the whole stimulus, I would say that it\'s, you know, it\'s a bigger, bigger task than Argument Part to a certain extent. And flaw\\n- 0:24has more guardrails. You know, there are like, there\'s more clues on what is bad in reasoning. You can memorize lists,\\n- 0:30you know, there\'s there\'s more to go on with Method. Like, yeah, you could maybe memorize like every single form of reasoning that\'s ever been given on the LSAT.\\n- 0:38I don\'t personally find that to be super valuable because it\'s just too much and there isn\'t enough return on investment to do it.\\n- 0:43You instead have to really read the room of the stimulus, understand on a deep level what the author was trying to do.\\n- 0:52I really like Method because it kind of plays to that sense of empathy that you have to have for the author of\\n- 0:57you want to read their intentions of like, OK, here was the technique you were trying to employ. And of course, with Method, they don\'t really want you to critique anything.\\n- 1:06I mean, I I critique it anyway in the CLIR because it\'s always good to have your critical hat on. But when you\'re in the answer choices itself, you\'re not like coming for their wig or anything.\\n- 1:17You\'re not like really trying to kill anybody. You\'re just describing what the author was doing, what their intention was, what they meant to do, a strategy they employed successful or not. But you\'re doing it without any sort of judgment or anything like that in the answer choices.\\n- 1:34And you\'re doing a very similar operation to what you do in Argument Part. And so all of the advice that I gave you on the Argument Part strategy video, it really pertains here.\\n- 1:42So if you haven\'t watched that one yet, definitely go back and watch the Argument Part strategy video because we\'re going to be building on it here.\\n- 1:47But the difference is it pertains to the Method pertains to the entire stimulus as opposed to one single Argument Part.\\n- 1:55Now, one thing I often see people have trouble with in Method is first off, the vocabulary of the answer choices, how they\'re phrased and being able to think in your Mission and your Method Mission in language like those answer choices.\\n- 2:09This tends to be really hard for people, which is why I do have a thing called the Method vocab list in the Loophole.\\n- 2:15And there\'s an uncut version as well online. And so I\'ll link to that here where you can actually go through and look at the uncut Method vocab list for more information.\\n- 2:22Of course, that\'s also relevant to Argument Part, but it\'s named after Method for a reason. You know, it was literally sourced from Method answer choices and so super, super helpful to use.\\n- 2:30Now, just one extra piece of advice about how to use the uncut Method vocab list and the Method vocab list that\'s in the Loophole.\\n- 2:38When you go through those those words, I want you to use the standard of could I easily use this word in a casual sentence?\\n- 2:48And if the answer to that question is no, you need to study that word and elevate it to that level\\n- 2:55because I think very often people don\'t like studying vocab and like don\'t want to have to do it.\\n- 2:59And so they\'re like, yeah, basically. And they\'ll write off the word in the list and be like, I don\'t need to pay attention to this anymore.\\n- 3:05But the standard that we have to employ is I know this word well enough that I could easily use it in a Mission.\\n- 3:11If you can\'t easily use one of the words in that vocab list in a Mission, you need to fricking study it and start using them in your everyday life.\\n- 3:19So, like really, to master Method questions, you want to master their language and their subject matter, which is forms of reasoning.\\n- 3:27And of course, this is not rocket science. It\'s stuff like employees a Counterexample against a general Principle. And of course, I\'m acting like, oh, yeah, of course, that\'s, you know, everything.\\n- 3:36But I understand that that\'s that\'s not how everybody talks all the time. And I\'ve gotten a lot of questions over the years of what a Counterexample like really, really is at, like an essential level.\\n- 3:48And of course, it\'s an example that is going against something that we said before. Or something that we\'re about to say.\\n- 3:54But it\'s an example against a claim. And it has to be an example and it has to be structured against.\\n- 3:59And I\'ve often been asked, well, could one of those be absent? No, it cannot be absent. But if you are not used to using these words in your everyday life, you want to challenge yourself to start employing them in conversations.\\n- 4:10I\'ll very often in tutoring give people a homework task of you have to use five Method vocab answer choice Method vocab list words in your everyday life every day.\\n- 4:21And you can pick which words and how you\'re going to do it and try and have a conversation with somebody in your life who is going to be understanding of you maybe being uncomfortable or messing things up. But you have to learn how to get those words in your mouth, because if you can\'t get\\n- 4:33those words in your mouth, you\'re always going to feel like a foreigner in the land of Method answer choices. And that sucks.\\n- 4:38No one wants that for you. And also when we have Method, flaw and Argument Part all utilizing that similar language, we just can\'t afford to have it be a foreign language, especially with the frequency with which flaw is asked, which, of course, we\'re going to get to in a minute.\\n- 4:53I wanted to also just go over some advanced Easter eggs on Method of things that they tend to talk about,\\n- 5:00just something that I really, really want to make sure that you know in Method questions and Argument Part as well, but especially Method. They really like to test whether you understand the difference between calling a claim false\\n- 5:13and calling it unjustified. And so I want to go over just what the two the difference between those two things really is.\\n- 5:20When I say something is false, that\'s equivalent to me saying Jenna did not go to that party. No. And or that the sky is not blue.\\n- 5:29The claim that the sky is blue is false. And that means the sky is anything other than blue. And like it\'s a very fact strong claim.\\n- 5:37And on the LSAT, they tend to not make as many falsehood claims. They tend to not say that\'s untrue. And when they do say that something\'s untrue, it\'s almost always flawed reasoning,\\n- 5:50you know, that because of the floor, we\'re about to get to in a second. The other side of calling something false is calling something unjustified.\\n- 5:59And what does it mean to say it\'s unjustified? It means you didn\'t prove this. This was not adequately demonstrated is another way they like to say it,\\n- 6:07meaning your premises didn\'t add up to actually prove this Conclusion. And they will test you in the Method answer choices on the difference between these two things of like demonstrates that a claim is false, demonstrates that a claim is unjustified.\\n- 6:19Those will be two different answers. And so in a stimulus to show a claim is unjustified, they\'ll be critiquing evidence\\n- 6:24and they\'ll be like, oh, yeah, see that that study was bad. This, you know, person was a bad sample or whatever. Therefore, this claim is unjustified.\\n- 6:33Therefore, the study does not actually prove what it thinks it\'s going to prove. That means it\'s unjustified, not that it is false.\\n- 6:40And so be really, really careful between those two things. And the reason why I said this is a flaw when they tend to say false in the Conclusion instead of unjustified is that\'s a possibility certainty error.\\n- 6:52Lack of evidence doesn\'t equal evidence of lacking, y\'all. I hope that you guys pick that up. But just one more thing to get over one of the biggest challenges of Method questions.\\n- 7:02If you are having trouble with Method, I really, really encourage you to just take out a half hour or something. And just read a bunch of Method answer choices. Make\\n- 7:13your own Method vocabulary list homegrown, home sourced. When you read those answers and you really translate them on sections you\'ve already seen, you don\'t need to be spoiling new sections or anything.\\n- 7:23You you start to get them more in your brain as a native language and you can take a level of curiosity with them where it\'s like, oh, you know, what is this? It would be nice for law school to be able to use these words as your native language.\\n- 7:34Like Method questions are honestly quite relevant to law school because you are going to have to describe forms of reasoning in your one L exams, which are going to, you know, control a lot of your future job prospects.\\n- 7:46And so this is not something you\'re doing just for the LSAT. This is something you\'re doing for not only your one L grades, but your future career as well.\\n- 7:52You\'re going to have to get with the program of how to use these words of like employing Counter examples to falsify claims, whatever else.\\n- 7:59And it\'s kind of do it now or do it later when your law school grades are on the line. I want to help you do it.\\n- 8:06And so I want to give you lots of resources to be able to do it. But you do have to put in that detail work time\\n- 8:12if this is not feeling supernatural for you. OK, I love you. I\'m excited. Go do more of that Method for Argument Part. Answer choice Translation spreadsheet, too.\\n\\n##### Day 12\\n\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\nWorkout\\n\\nDecode the most difficult abstract answer choice patterns\\n\\n### [Method/Flaw/Argument Part Answer Choice Translation Spreadsheet](https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/workouts/method-flaw-argument-part-answer-choice-translation-spreadsheet)\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\n[\\n\\nConcept Review\\n\\n](https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/concept-review)\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"The Necessary Condition Trick • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/learn/video/the-necessary-condition-trick?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## The Necessary Condition Trick\\n\\n0:27\\n\\n/\\n\\n8:10\\n\\n- 0:00The necessary condition trick. If I can adequately explain this and even remotely get you on the hype train for the necessary condition trick in this video, it will be one of the single greatest accomplishments of my life.\\n- 0:10Because I\'ve been trying and failing to explain this and get people on the hype train for like 10 plus years.\\n- 0:15And so let\'s go. Today\'s the frickin\' day. So what we\'re going to do is just a brief example. I made like a baby stimulus of my own design just to illustrate this concept. Because I honestly think it\'s the easiest way to get someone on the hype train is to see it actually in action, how effective\\n- 0:30this trick is. And so I don\'t want to give you too much explanation first. I\'d rather just show you. So let me just translate quick CLIR this stimulus and then we\'ll get into some\\n- 0:37juicy made up answer choices that are hiding on the next page. All right. Insulated mugs cost only a few dollars to produce.\\n- 0:44Okay, so an insulated mug, it only costs a couple of bucks to produce. Which like yeah, like Stanley cups are probably like super cheap to make somewhere.\\n- 0:50But market manipulation has caused prices to rise far beyond typical markups. Okay, but market manipulation has caused price to rise like far beyond a typical markup.\\n- 0:58It\'s like going way, way up. And it\'s like, yeah, these trendy Stanley cups, they make like, you know, probably mad bank on the prices on these things are super cheap to make.\\n- 1:05So therefore the insulated mug market has become untenable. Okay, so the insulated mug market, it\'s become untenable as like, what if it\'s super tenable to just raise your prices on and on down the line on something trendy, right?\\n- 1:17Just because prices are going way up beyond what\'s typical, that doesn\'t necessarily make it an untenable market. Like super tenable business business practice, you know,\\n- 1:26if until it doesn\'t work, if it\'s working, but hypothetically could work on into infinity. So why not? All right.\\n- 1:33So that\'s my CLIR. And now we want to go into our pretend question because we\'ve got our lovely little CLIR.\\n- 1:40Okay, wonderful. Oh, I\'ve had magic behave. Okay. So all right. Principle Conform question. Now I know it\'s Principle Conform. I want something Provable and I\'m thinking, okay, what would underlie this stimulus?\\n- 1:54Okay. In a given situation in which prices are rising beyond what\'s typical, then that market in which that occurs may be untenable or something, something like that,\\n- 2:04that kind of incorporates an underlying feeling underneath the premises and into the Conclusion. Remember Principle Conform is different from just any garden variety Strengthen or Sufficient Assumption question.\\n- 2:16It has to be Provable underlying the stimulus, not just any random thing that proves the stimulus Conclusion true as in Sufficient Assumption or any random thing that makes the Conclusion more likely as in Strengthen.\\n- 2:28And so, all right, let\'s go into our fake answer choices, everyone. So a if production costs increase, a market is tenable. Okay. So if production costs go way up, then that market is tenable.\\n- 2:40And so ding, ding, ding, ding, ding, there\'s something here that I really want to point out. As soon as I see a market is tenable, I absolutely know this answer is wrong.\\n- 2:49100, 100, 100. And the reason for that is my Conclusion, as that was a really bad line, my Conclusion is that the market has become untenable.\\n- 2:59And when that necessary condition doesn\'t match the Conclusion, it\'s the negation, that answer has to be wrong. The really, really cool thing about Principle Conform is the Conclusion and the necessary condition of the answer, if the answer choice is conditional at all, will\\n- 3:16very, very, very likely match. And if they give you a conditional, but it doesn\'t match, like they say put the Conclusion in the sufficient or they put a negation of the Conclusion in the necessary condition, that answer will always be wrong.\\n- 3:30And so here, this stimulus was not trying to prove that a given market was tenable, right? And so it\'s absolutely impossible for this answer to ever be right.\\n- 3:40And the cool thing about this is it\'s a really nice time saving trick that you can be absolutely sure of because it works by the rules of logic. You know, it\'s not just some kind of like random proxy metric that might work most of the time.\\n- 3:52It works by the rules of logic that like if they put a negation of the Conclusion in the necessary condition of an answer on Principle Conform,\\n- 4:00it\'s wrong for sure. And so here we aren\'t trying to prove in this Conclusion that a market was tenable. And so this is not Provable.\\n- 4:07And then just to beat up on answer A a little bit more, this sufficient condition that we\'ve got right here, if production costs increase, is actually dormant.\\n- 4:17That is a Dormant Conditional answer choice, because notice in our Conclusion here about the production costs, there was no increase, right?\\n- 4:25It was just that production costs are low, but there was actually no change whatsoever. And so we cannot activate this sufficient condition.\\n- 4:31And if we can\'t activate the sufficient condition with a premise from the stimulus, it\'s dormant and it\'s again always wrong.\\n- 4:38And so we double, triple, quadruple now. A is incorrect. All right, let\'s go to B. So if a market becomes untenable, markups must have increased beyond what is typical.\\n- 4:47Okay, so if a market has become untenable, then markups on the products must have increased beyond what\'s typical. And so here I know this is wrong\\n- 4:55as certainly as I know that A is wrong. Why? Because it placed the Conclusion in the sufficient condition. And this is a big issue. Because if I\'m on a Provable Question, and you know, just really any argument, the Conclusion is not a fact I can use to go start activating conditionals in the\\n- 5:14answer choices. And what does it mean to activate a conditional? It means that I know that sufficient condition is true.\\n- 5:19I am activating it with a fact from the stimulus. The Conclusion is something I\'m trying to prove, but you can think of it as it\'s kind of in this like nebulous am I proven state.\\n- 5:28It\'s not a fact that I can then go and use to activate other sufficient conditions. Otherwise, I\'m a dirty circular reasoner who\'s assuming my Conclusion is true to go and do other things.\\n- 5:39My Conclusion is the thing. Remember, it\'s like the cake that I have to keep off the ground. And so that cake is like pretty helpless.\\n- 5:44It\'s not a fact that I could go and use to activate sufficient conditions whatsoever. And so here this if a market becomes untenable, immediate\\n- 5:54no. Absolutely not. I cannot put my Conclusion in the sufficient condition. And just for all my homies out there with deep knowledge, yes, this is a reversed if our premises, then our Conclusion answer choice. That it put the Conclusion in the sufficient and the premises in the necessary.\\n- 6:11And you know, we can\'t do that. If our premises, then our Conclusion is in the order it\'s in for a reason.\\n- 6:16If our premises, sufficient condition premises, then our Conclusion, necessary condition Conclusion, that placement is super important. B\'s got it reversed.\\n- 6:25And so we say no to that. All right, so let\'s go to answer C. If markups in a given market have increased beyond what is typical, said market has become untenable.\\n- 6:33Okay, so if markups have gone beyond what is typical in a given market, then that market has become untenable. So things you notice here, right?\\n- 6:39Market has become untenable. This matches our Conclusion, doesn\'t it? Ding, ding, ding, ding, ding, check mark, check mark, check mark, victory for us.\\n- 6:49It matches our Conclusion. And I\'m not saying like every single answer choice in which the necessary condition matches the Conclusion on Principle Conform is always going to be right no matter what.\\n- 7:00Because of course, yeah, we have to still, you know, have this sufficient condition be activatable by the stimulus. You know, if A\'s sufficient condition were here on answer C down here, well then that the answer C would be wrong if we used A\'s sufficient condition.\\n- 7:15But the sufficient condition on answer A actually can be activated by the stimulus, right? Because answer C\'s sufficient condition is one of the premises.\\n- 7:24And so here we have that nice if our premises, then our Conclusion construction, which is, you know, both Powerful and Provable.\\n- 7:32It\'s a wonderful, wonderful little hack. But I love the necessary condition trick as well because of the impact it has on the wrong answers and the certainty that you can have that those answers are wrong within such a quick period of time.\\n- 7:45Okay, I hope that this helps. I hope that you would feel also quite comfortable choosing answer C if you were in the driver\'s seat of this iPad.\\n- 7:52But give me your feedback. I\'m always happy to make more videos about the necessary condition trick if this one still didn\'t hit and I\'m, you know, in my infinite quest for, I don\'t know, the Fountain of Youth, Pot of Golds, whatever, a trail at the end of the rainbow.\\n- 8:07But I will see you very soon. I hope to hear from you.\\n\\n##### Day 13\\n\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\n\\nIf one commits an action that causes harm, then one is an immoral actor.\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\n[\\n\\nConcept Review\\n\\n](https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/concept-review)\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"The Worst Conditional Habit Everyone Has • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/learn/video/the-worst-conditional-habit-everyone-has\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## The Worst Conditional Habit Everyone Has\\n\\n0:11\\n\\n/\\n\\n3:48\\n\\n- 0:00All right, the worst conditional habit everybody has. You\'re probably expecting it to be like some super technical, mathy sounding thing of like, uh, the consequence. It\'s like really not that.\\n- 0:13The worst conditional habit everyone has is that they are very all or nothing about like an exclusive relationship between diagramming and understanding.\\n- 0:23Very few people will actually combine a conceptual understanding of the stimulus\'s Translation with a diagramming level understanding. Typically if someone is going to diagram, they will want to skip straight to diagram and diagramming and not really think about what the sentence means in any kind\\n- 0:41of conceptual way that they will kind of let the diagramming do that step for them. But here\'s the problem with that.\\n- 0:47If you let the diagramming do the understanding step for you, then your diagram might end up wrong. Um, your diagram might not actually represent the real meaning of the sentence or phrase that you\'re trying to diagram.\\n- 1:00And so then Xing out that moment of conceptual Translation before you diagram, it really just creates a very faulty picture on all but the most simple conditionals.\\n- 1:13And very often, I think the reason why this happens is, you know, time, but beyond the, the time panic aspect of it is people will not know their conditional indicators typically well enough to be able to easily rephrase them into a conceptual Translation\\n- 1:30that translating a sentence that contains the word only if into a personalized if then feels harder in the moment than just going and jumping to a diagram or they aren\'t sure of their indicators at all, which is really a separate problem. You know, maybe that\'s the second biggest\\n- 1:46diagramming habit people have that\'s bad is not really knowing their indicators. But if you let\'s say you do, you still might be not quite comfortable enough with translating them into the real meaning of the phrase\\n- 2:00rather than just like beep beep boop robot. I put thing after arrow, you know, like that, that is like a typically a lesser amount of understanding than actually being able to frame the conceptual relationship between the two variables yourself in a personalized if then before you put the\\n- 2:19diagram down because you really want to understand what the statement that they wrote, what it really means to then know that your conditional is right.\\n- 2:28Very often we have a lot of anxiety about like, what if my conditional is wrong? I don\'t want a diagram because what if I put it down wrong\\n- 2:34and all of that different kind of stuff? And honestly, this whole conceptual Translation step of really feeling out what they mean by the statement they give you, that\'s before you diagram. That\'s the way to know that your conditional is right. Because if you actually translated into a personalized if then\\n- 2:51before you throw the diagram down, then you\'ll know that, OK, did what I write match my personalized if then because I generated my personalized if then Translation on the basis of whatever it is that they wrote.\\n- 3:05And so then you kind of have that intermediary checkpoint phase. And of course, that personalized if then it has to match the meaning of whatever words they gave you in the stimulus\\n- 3:13and it has to match what you put down on the diagram. But it\'s that nice little middle man to just kind of double check each side.\\n- 3:20And so I hope that this helps. I hope that this encourages you to diagram in the quip stage of your Translation sentence by sentence, not putting off your diagram and all the way to the end of the stimulus and then probably declining to do it because it costs too much time.\\n- 3:34It doesn\'t actually cost that much time. It\'s just the same as taking a note. And I hope that it has you actually think about the meaning of the phrases you\'re diagramming before you throw them down on the paper again in that quip stage of the Translation. All right.\\n- 3:47Hope this helped and I\'ll see you soon.\\n\\n##### In This Training Block\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\n[\\n\\nConcept Review\\n\\n](https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/concept-review)\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"Necessary Assumption Question Strategy • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/learn/video/necessary-assumption-question-strategy?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\n\\nWe could get a variety of fruits to fill this fruit bowl for a painting we\'re going to make, but then if we get a variety of fruits, we\'ll have to include apples. Therefore, having a variety of fruits in the bowl is too expensive.\\n\\n***Versions of the Necessary Assumption Back Up Plan***\\n\\n1. If the conclusion is true, does this have to be true?\\n2. Does that author have to believe this?\\n3. Does the author have to think this is true?\\n4. If the conclusion is proven, does this have to be true?\\n5. If the premises worked to prove the conclusion, is this required?\\n6. If these premises proved this conclusion, what must the author assume?\\n7. Does the argument rely on this?\\n8. For the argument to make any sense, does this have to be its foundation?\\n9. What must the author believe to think they actually proved this conclusion?\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 1.3 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"bf7d87","wall_time_seconds":0.26826,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3771,"output":"FILE: Dont Hesitate Between the Steps • LO.md\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"Dont Hesitate Between the Steps • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/dont-hesitate-between-the-steps?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## Don\'t Hesitate Between the Steps\\n\\n0:54\\n\\n/\\n\\n3:56\\n\\n- 0:00Don\'t hesitate between the steps. This can be a huge source of Translation, uh, and CLIR time lag where you are unable to hit your time goal for much longer because you\'re hesitating between the steps.\\n- 0:13But, hesitating between the steps also means you aren\'t really doing the process as prescribed. And so, in, you know, the ideal world, the Translation CLIR process is your thinking process.\\n- 0:24There is no not, like, another governing process on top of the Translation CLIR process. And so, we want everything that\'s coming out of your mouth as you\'re translating and CLIRing to be your raw unfiltered thoughts.\\n- 0:37You shouldn\'t have a pause where you\'re like, oh, let me come up with a good CLIR. Oh, what about this?\\n- 0:42What about this all silently? Because all of that processing should be your out loud- well. Like, quipping, and things like that.\\n- 0:50If you\'re thinking before you come up with a good quip- that thinking actually is your quip. You know? But the issue is, if that thinking is not actually directly engaging with your Translation, engaging with the content of the stimulus, it\'s not very good quipping.\\n- 1:03It\'s not very useful quipping. And so, we need to just exnay all of that more like kind of meta on top of the situation strategic thinking of like We\'ll see Oh, what could a good quip be?\\n- 1:14I can\'t think of a good quip. Watch the I should disappear video. The I should disappear. Uhh, like, you don\'t want to have yourself at all involved in the process where it\'s like, oh, I\'m thinking of a quip right now.\\n- 1:27No. Like, you need to be in there with, you know, the fish, the coral, the Pacific Ocean, whatever the stimulus or passage is about, those are the only things- that should be entering into your mind while you\'re translating, quipping, and CLIRing.\\n- 1:40Umm, there should be no, like, I\'m a person sitting in my desk and I really need to meet this Translation CLIR time goal.\\n- 1:45Like, no, that-that-that should not be part of our- our reality at all. The only thing that should be part of our reality is the coral, the fish, and the ocean.\\n- 1:52And so, if you\'re having those sorts of intrusive management thoughts, get them out of here. Think-think of it more, like, competitive improv.\\n- 2:00Like, I think I say in some other videos that I have done improv comedy for a long time, or at least I did.\\n- 2:06I haven\'t done it a long time now. But, well, I grew up doing it for a really long time. And, it-in-in, improv, there\'s a rule that you-you have to just keep going.\\n- 2:14You have to just say yes and. You can\'t, like, stop the scene and be like, sorry guys, I need to think.\\n- 2:18You know, you-you have to continue on, no matter what. And the rules of improv really apply to the rules of Translation equipped- and we cannot afford to have you editing your own thoughts before you have them, because that process of editing is a thought, and we need the Translation CLIR process to\\n- 2:33Be all of your thoughts. This has to be really an off-the-dome sort of process, and you know what? If you say something wrong or bad, so do I sometimes.\\n- 2:45So does everybody. If you say something wrong or bad, it\'s much better to be- fearlessly wrong, fearlessly bad, and just make the mistake as opposed to making a mistake every single time, because you\'re so worried you\'re gonna make a mistake, because all of this pausing and hesitating between the steps\\n- 3:00And being like, oh, but how do I come up with a good quip? That is itself a mistake, and so you don\'t want your, you know, fear that you\'re gonna do something non-optimal to keep you from excellence, because it will.\\n- 3:11And it is- It\'s completely okay that you do something non-optimal and quip one time. You\'re not great at it on your quips, you know, like, obviously we\'re all trying to get better, trying to become excellent over time, but it doesn\'t need to be a Mona Lisa, every single, every single quip, and\\n- 3:27Movement is life, freezing is death. Say it with me. Movement is life, freezing is death. So we always need to keep it moving.\\n- 3:34Keep it pushing. Don\'t stop. Doesn\'t matter. Just- Say something. And even if you\'re saying, uhh, don\'t know, don\'t know, don\'t know, say that!\\n- 3:41Rather than being like, hmm, what could a Translation be? No, that\'s just waste time. It\'s dead air. No dead air.\\n- 3:51No dead air rule. Uhh, and so, go go go. I believe in you. I know you can do it, and I\'ll see you soon.\\n\\n##### Day 4\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\nFILE: How and Why to Do Translation Plus CLIR Drills • LO.md\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"How and Why to Do Translation Plus CLIR Drills • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/how-and-why-to-do-translation-plus-clir-drills?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\n\\n***Translation + CLIR Drills***\\n\\n1. Read and translate the stimulus in chunks.\\n2. Quip each chunk of the stimulus after you translate.\\n3. Continue this process on every stimulus chunk until you reach the end of stimulus.\\n4. Immediately proceed to the CLIR after your final quip of the stimulus (or the final quip is the CLIR).\\n\\n***Translation + CLIR Drill Tips***\\n\\n- Perform all steps of the drill out loud, and record your drills with a voice recorder.\\n\\t- You will eventually transition this process to silent, but the time goals are built around an outloud process.\\n- Press \\"record\\" at the very beginning, and do not end the recording until you have finished with the entire drill.\\n- Do not pause or stop the recording between steps or stimuli.\\n- No hyperskipping on Translation + CLIR Drills.\\n\\n***Translation + CLIR Goals***\\n\\n- The time goal for Translation + CLIR is 20 minutes.\\n- The accommodated time goal for Translation + CLIR is 30 minutes (or 25 minutes as an ideal stretch goal).\\n- Meeting this time goal is only valid under two conditions:\\n\\t- You have a legitimate CLIR for every stimulus.\\n\\t\\t- You have translated and quipped every stimulus in the section.\\r\\nFILE: How and Why to Quip the Answer Choices • LO.md\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"How and Why to Quip the Answer Choices • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/how-and-why-to-quip-the-answer-choices?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## How and Why to Quip the Answer Choices\\n\\n0:07\\n\\n/\\n\\n6:38\\n\\n- 0:00how and why to quip the answer choices. This is one of those things that essentially no one wants to do.\\n- 0:06Nobody wants to quip the answer choices, but the quipping of the answer choices to me is actually the highlight slash most important moment in the doing of any question,\\n- 0:18because the quipping of the answer choices is when you get the chance to figure out whether that answer is right with actual, you know, real data as opposed to just kind of skipping to the end.\\n- 0:30And so let me explain. Let me explain. I want people to quip the answers in a certain way.\\n- 0:35Obviously, you read the answer, you translate the answer, you get to that quipping stage. In that quipping stage, ideally, you\'re going to quip that answer through melding together the information from the stimulus with the answer choice\\n- 0:48and having some sort of commentary that\'s very much informed by the stimulus. You\'re bringing to bear a lot of the concepts, even the phrases that are included in the\\n- 0:58you bring them down into the answer choices for that little roundtable discussion that just involves you and no one else at a roundtable.\\n- 1:04And in that moment, you are conceptually engaging with the answer choice according to what the question is really asking,\\n- 1:12but using evidence from the stimulus to have that interesting discussion. And so when you\'re having that interesting discussion, you\'re actually forming the basis of evidence for a judgment.\\n- 1:23And it\'s really important. As we would kind of know from just the LSAT and like circular reasoning and things, it\'s really important to have evidence for a judgment or a claim or a Conclusion before you go to that judgment, claim or Conclusion.\\n- 1:39And this presumption, which I feel like we can all agree on, right? You would rather start with evidence and then move toward a claim rather than starting with a claim and moving toward evidence.\\n- 1:50You\'re just more likely to get a valid claim or Conclusion out of that sort of arrangement, right? When you start with evidence rather than starting with the claim.\\n- 1:59However, when most people engage with LSAT answer choices, they do not want to start with evidence. In fact, they want to read it very read the answer choice very quickly, silently, and they want to immediately have a right wrong judgment.\\n- 2:12And that right wrong judgment has to be, I guess, informed by like magic or intuition or liking or disliking or all kinds of things that are not actual metrics or actual evidence.\\n- 2:24They will see when people do demos and things like that on video, they\'ll be like, oh, you have to know the fate of the answer immediately.\\n- 2:31You can\'t have any sort of processing of the answer. You have to just read it and immediately and intuitively know if it\'s right or wrong.\\n- 2:37And while that is a wonderful goal and perhaps it is something that could happen on some answer choices in the future.\\n- 2:43And it\'s something like, I know what that feels like. It\'s not like I\'m saying it doesn\'t exist. If you were right now trying to remedy a big score gap, doing an impression of somebody who\'s at like expert level, who made a video and instantly and intuitively saw why an answer is right or wrong, doing\\n- 3:00an impression of them where you read an answer and you\'re like, yeah, so it\'s wrong. That\'s not going to get you anywhere\\n- 3:06because you\'re right there forcing knowledge that you don\'t actually have. You\'re forcing a judgment that you don\'t actually really have data for. The\\n- 3:15people feel so much pressure to make that judgment really quickly that they skip the information analysis stage. And that\'s what really the quipping of the answer choices is.\\n- 3:26It\'s that information analysis stage in which like this is the meat of doing the question, right? I mean, this is when you take the answer choice and you\'re like, hey, does this actually function\\n- 3:36according to the question stem in regards to the stimulus? And in a way, what I\'m asking there of like the level of engagement I\'m asking for, it is asking, is this right?\\n- 3:45But it\'s not asking, is this right? By literally saying the words, is this right? Because the words, is this right? Is like way too referential, way too generalized.\\n- 3:54It\'s going to lead to very kind of cheap answer choices of like, I don\'t like this. This one\'s good. This one could be it.\\n- 4:00Notice that there\'s like no content to those answers. They\'re like mass market generalized quips. Like I talked about in my last video where you could just pick them up and put them on a different stimulus or not a different stimulus, a different answer choice. And they would make\\n- 4:15just as much sense. You want to stay away from that sort of quipping because it means you aren\'t really engaging with what you read and you don\'t have real evidence that\'s informed by specificity.\\n- 4:24And you might be saying to yourself like, oh, you know, how do I have time for this? I\'m going to go over that in another video. So don\'t freak out about that quite yet.\\n- 4:30But I will tell you that when you see my question, my question stem demos and my question doing demos, you\'ll see me go through answer choices. And I did a speed run at one point. You can go watch the speed run if you want, where I went through and I actually tried to do a section\\n- 4:46out loud in 35 minutes. And the one thing I gave myself perMission to do was I wasn\'t going to translate the answers out loud because I was like, OK, doing that out loud in 35, you know, it\'s a lot slower to talk things out in general. And so I know I\'m not going to be able to do it if I translate\\n- 4:58the answers out loud. Some of them I still did just, you know, it was intuitive. It felt like the right thing to do in the moment. But one thing you\'ll notice is I\'m still clipping those answers out loud.\\n- 5:06That didn\'t go anywhere because that is the fundamentally important action of every question is actually talking through the evidence for and against a given answer choices correctness in regard to an exploration and investigation through curiosity of like, how does this engage with the question\\n- 5:24through the lens of the stimulus? Like that\'s what I want to find out. So when you see me in those videos, even in the speed run, but then, of course, in the longer question type explanations as well, you\'ll see me engage with every answer in this way. And so I really want you to pay close attention\\n- 5:39when you watch my demos of how I\'m engaging with those answer choices. I\'m actually not just doing a big explanation or something.\\n- 5:46I\'m telling you how I\'m thinking about the answer right after I translated it. And that way I\'m thinking about the answer is what then leads me to my verdict on that answer choice.\\n- 5:55And so if you remember one thing from this video, don\'t do circular reasoning where you decide the Conclusion and then you just, you know, try and a priori justify it using the fact that it\'s right to show that it\'s right or wrong to show that it\'s wrong. So\\n- 6:09that isn\'t going to get you to high accuracy on this test. Instead, translate the answer, equip the answer in regards to the stimulus, bringing information from the stimulus down into context, have a conversation with the answer through the lens of the question type.\\n- 6:22I think I\'ve said the stimulus and the question type are both the lens. You can do it either way.\\n- 6:27And going from there to determine what is right and what is wrong. All right. I hope that this helped. I hope it provided a little more specificity on exactly how clipping in the answers works and why it\'s so valuable. Thank\\n- 6:37you.\\n\\n##### Day 2\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"9eae9b","wall_time_seconds":0.2013535,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":340,"output":"---\\ntitle: \\"Method Flaw Argument Part Answer Choice Translation Spreadsheet • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/workouts/method-flaw-argument-part-answer-choice-translation-spreadsheet\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## Instructions\\n\\n1. Make a spreadsheet with three columns, or use [this template](https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1V7BS4BgrznJf_JQepzRK07dJw5yX0dZijLFSFYDqank/edit?usp=sharing):\\n\\t1. **Column 1**: Vexing Answer Choice\\n\\t\\t2. **Column 2**: Answer Choice Translation\\n\\t\\t3. **Column 3**: Corresponding Stimulus\\n2. Collect all the difficult answer choices to translate, focusing especially on Method/Flaw/Argument Part answer choices.\\n\\t1. This could include harvesting answer choices from your sections immediately after you\'re done or going back over old sections to harvest answer choices.\\n3. Write the difficult answers in the Vexing Answer Choice column of the spreadsheet, one answer choice per row.\\n4. Translate the answers in the Answer Choice Translation column of the spreadsheet.\\n5. In the Corresponding Stimulus column of the spreadsheet, write an example stimulus which would make the answer choice in that row correct.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"5142cc","wall_time_seconds":0.2785076,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":257,"output":"corpus/Curriculum\\\\Necessary Assumption\\\\Notes\\\\_scan_Sabrina review.md\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Necessary Assumption\\\\Notes\\\\_scan_Sabrina between Joshua  and Sabrina  - 2026_01_27 19_18 EST - Notes by Gemini.md\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Necessary Assumption\\\\Notes\\\\_scan_Sabrina - 2026_02_21 16_46 EST - Notes by Gemini.md\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Necessary Assumption\\\\Notes\\\\_scan_Sabrina ( review Nov9th).md\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Necessary Assumption\\\\Notes\\\\_megadoc_notes_necessary_assumption.md\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Conditional Logic\\\\Notes\\\\_megadoc_reference_conditionals.md\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Conditional Logic\\\\Notes\\\\_megadoc_other_conditionals.md\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Conditional Logic\\\\Notes\\\\_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Conditional Logic\\\\Notes\\\\_megadoc_lecture_conditionals.md\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Conditional Logic\\\\Notes\\\\homework_conditionals.md\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Flaw and Famous Flaws\\\\Notes\\\\_megadoc_other_flaw.md\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Flaw and Famous Flaws\\\\Notes\\\\reference_flaw.md\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Flaw and Famous Flaws\\\\Notes\\\\notes_flaw.md\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.5 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"be6668","wall_time_seconds":0.3109149,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":9992,"output":"Warning: truncated output (original token count: 9992)\\nTotal output lines: 604\\n\\nFILE: Micro Quips and Stream of Consciousness Magic • LO.md\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"Micro Quips and Stream of Consciousness Magic • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/micro-quips-and-stream-of-consciousness-magic?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## Micro-Quips and Stream of Consciousness Magic\\n\\n0:24\\n\\n/\\n\\n7:59\\n\\n- 0:00Ah, microquips and stream of consciousness magic. Yeah, microquips are so fun. Microquips are basically where you are at any point in the RTQ stage,\\n- 0:09maybe not in the Q stage, because it would make no sense to have a microquip inside the stage, but at some point in the reading or translating stage. And you just have this like moment of like a burst of inspiration. And you just you have a thought and you need to say it\\n- 0:22like that\'s a microquip. It\'s like a little kind of like bubble, like a little carbonated pop in the middle of another step.\\n- 0:30If you really feel that reaction in that moment of like, OK, yeah, sure. You know, like whatever weird thing you want to say in that moment back at what they\'re saying. If you feel like it\'s natural and you can\'t hold it in, just say it right in the moment.\\n- 0:43However, I really want to advise you not to let these microquips, these like tiny little baby quips that pop up in different parts of the process. They do not replace the Q stage of the RTQ process.\\n- 0:54And so sometimes when you watch my examples, you are going to see these little microquip moments because I like honestly can\'t help it.\\n- 1:00Sometimes when I\'m reading, I just need to say something. And sometimes you even when I was doing my one example, I was like, I just displayed very bad form because I quit right before my Translation.\\n- 1:09That was a microquip. That\'s like these little things that they just come to mind and you just want to say them. But they are not a replacement for the quip stage of the RTQ process.\\n- 1:18You can think of the quip stage of the RTQ process that occurs after the Translation stage of the RTQ,\\n- 1:23the RT, I guess, that that\'s like when you\'re really processing a lot of raw information and you need to have that processing stage to just make sure that everything is really nicely incorporated.\\n- 1:35And if you have these little microquips otherwise, that\'s totally OK. That\'s just you honoring your stream of consciousness technique where you aren\'t editing yourself, you aren\'t vetting. Oh, was this a good quip, a bad quip?\\n- 1:46Was it OK? Was it useful? All that like not doing all that means sometimes you\'re going to have a little stream of consciousness magic moment where a quip pops up in the middle of something else and you just honor it and you just go with it.\\n- 1:56And it\'s incorporated into the process and totally fine. But I don\'t want you to take that as this kind of license to not do a real quip at the end, because those real quips are really more substantive and they allow that kind of preCLIR motion. They allow you to just move forward into the next\\n- 2:10steps of the process. So we don\'t want to cut those out just because we have microquips. But I want to offer you one little thing to remember in terms of why quipping should be stream of consciousness.\\n- 2:20And what do I mean by stream of consciousness? I mean, it\'s whatever thoughts come into your mind in the moment. You just say them, you just think them.\\n- 2:28We don\'t want you like I\'ve talked about before, prethinking your reactions as like, are they good enough reactions? If not, I say something else.\\n- 2:35No, no, no, no. The reaction is the reaction. And you just move on and you keep on, you know, coming up with better ones. But you don\'t opt out.\\n- 2:41A good rule of thumb for this is an adaptation of an Ernest Hemingway quote. Ernest Hemingway has a quote called, not called, I guess he said, write drunk, edit sober. And I used to have this as a little illustrated postcard behind my desk when I was writing the Loophole. And no, I was\\n- 2:58not literally drunk when I wrote the Loophole. But the the message behind write drunk, edit sober is that we are in that writing idea generation stage. You cannot be going and like vetting what you did, trying to make the perfect sentence, rewriting one sentence over and over and\\n- 3:14over again, trying to make it, you know, ideal. Instead, you need to just get those ideas out. You need to write drunk, you know, just go do it.\\n- 3:22And you know what? You\'ll fix it later. And edit sober, meaning that sober stage where you\'re editing, you\'re like, OK, here\'s where I clean up my own mess.\\n- 3:29Like that was kind of a disaster in certain ways. And I enforce this for myself with the Loophole. And like I could literally show you, I mean, it\'s not this notebook, but it\'s a notebook that kind of look like this one\\n- 3:38that I I wrote the whole Loophole book out longhand in notebooks handwritten. Yes, the Loophole is four hundred sixty two pages.\\n- 3:49And yes, I did actually handwrite the entire book. The first draft of every page of the book outside of like the real else, that drills. I didn\'t rewrite all the stimuli or anything,\\n- 3:59although I did. I think I rewrote the examples. But all of those were originally handwritten in my notebook.\\n- 4:05And you want to know why? It was because it was only in my notebook without any of the electronic garbage to distract you that I could truly write drunk,\\n- 4:13that I could truly actually just keep on going. And you can\'t really edit when you\'re writing, you know, in a big pen and a paper notebook,\\n- 4:19you could, but it would be pretty non-ideal. And I I loved the flow state that I would get into where I would just write and write and write.\\n- 4:26And I knew when I put it into the computer, I\'m going to edit it. I\'m going to fix whatever is broken.\\n- 4:31Now, my extended metaphor or analogy over to Hemingway, it has a purpose because the analogous case here is quip drunk, CLIR sober. And\\n- 4:42when you\'re quipping, just get it out. Just have the idea, you know, just have the thought. And maybe it gets you somewhere.\\n- 4:50Maybe it doesn\'t. Maybe it\'s a dead end. Maybe it isn\'t. But you know what? You\'re energized. You\'re engaged. You\'re doing something in the stimulus.\\n- 4:56You\'re bringing your beautiful, unique analytical mind to bear on the information as you read and translate. And that\'s what I want.\\n- 5:03That\'s what I care about. I have made a lot of, you know, explanation videos in my life. And I don\'t think I can ever think of a time where I was like, you know what?\\n- 5:12I didn\'t like that quip. I don\'t want to share that. Like, no, because in a sense, as long as you are actively engaged in quick quipping specifically and like in in with the actual thoughts, then there really aren\'t bad quips or right quips.\\n- 5:27Like, sure, we can offer you pro tips of like things that make quips even more optimized or even more better, all that different kind of stuff.\\n- 5:33But there\'s nothing that\'s going to teach you that better than experience of feeling out how quipping can grow across the stimulus, how you can reference earlier parts of the stimulus in a later quip and really make connections or show where connections don\'t exist.\\n- 5:45The best way for you to learn that is to go and get in the ring yourself, actually be in the arena.\\n- 5:52As Teddy Roosevelt once said, I love to. I need to get a thing for back there. But there\'s nothing better than being in the arena and watching all the tips in the world\\n- 6:01isn\'t going to do anything if you don\'t actually get in there and challenge yourself very, very directly. And so in the CLIR stage, though, that\'s when we become sober editors.\\n- 6:10Because, yeah, you\'re CLIR as to like actually like be a Loophole or be an Inference or be a Resolution, be a Controversy, whatever. And of course, I do allow a lot of flexibility in the CLIR.\\n- 6:20Like if you want to say a Necessary Assumption, it\'s devolvable. OK, cool. But there\'s a lot more success and failure on that.\\n- 6:25Like, you know, there\'s a CLIR answer key. There\'s ways to gauge whether a CLIR is successful or not successful in more of a very directly palpable sense than you would have with a quip.\\n- 6:35A quip is meant to be that creative exploration stage. You\'re putting ideas together and it\'s like there are no bad ideas and improv.\\n- 6:41Maybe you watch that YouTube video where I show you there are bad ideas and improv, but there are no bad ideas and improv in an LSAT land.\\n- 6:44And so I want to let you be free in that sense. And then when you get into the CLIR, that\'s when you\'ve got to, you know, batten down the hatches, button things up and actually start to edit and make sure that all of your analysis is going to a place.\\n- 6:58And so I hope that that helped. I hope that I\'ve encouraged you to access your stream of consciousness magic because stream of consciousness magic is truly great.\\n- 7:06I mean, you might have guessed this. So forgive me if I\'m telling you something you already know. But all the videos you see in this whole loop online land are improvised.\\n- 7:17And that\'s like, you know, tens of hours a video or all. No, no script, no bullets. It\'s all improvised because that stream of consciousness magic,\\n- 7:29like that\'s what I that\'s the way I can do the best I can for you is by actually being present with you in this moment and talking to you, you know, by myself in my office, but talking to you as much as I can.\\n- 7:40That\'s the energy, that awakeness, that kind of alertness and engaged feeling. That\'s how I want you to be when you\'re with a stimulus, not like accessing some pre-programmed strategy to quip or something like that.\\n- 7:51I want you to take in information and I want you to react to it in the moment, just like I\'m reacting to you right now without you being even here.\\n- 7:55All right. I love you. And I miss you, even though I don\'t know you. All right. See you soon. All right.\\n\\n##### Day 2\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\nFILE: PT 111 S 3 Q 1 Translation Plus CLIR • LO.md\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"PT 111 S 3 Q 1 Translation Plus CLIR • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/pt-111-s-3-q-1-translation-plus-clir?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## PT111.S3.Q1 Translation + CLIR\\n\\n##### Day 2\\n\\n![](https://vz-3500a044-c45.b-cdn.net/bcdn_token=HS256-CjYGY15vl3INZ1HARrQu6aRVWL7W_uCJZK7WuePOYv0&token_path=%2Fb01b2882-b4ac-4a14-99f6-13254a8ae419%2F&expires=1788480000/b01b2882-b4ac-4a14-99f6-13254a8ae419/thumbnail_2994084f.jpg)\\n\\n##### [What is Reading? And so I\'m just going to run to a stimulus and I will show you exactly what it looks like to RTQ individual pieces of text.\\n- 0:11And so each sentence that I read, or if I wanted to cut it sooner and just do a portion of a sentence, that would also be okay.\\n- 0:17I will read it, I will translate it, and I will quip it, and then I will repeat that process on the next chunk of text that I get to.\\n- 0:23And you\'ll see here that it can actually turn into a nice fluid process. And so I\'m just going to go through it and do it in a nice, slowed down way, the way I normally would.\\n- 0:31And then after I\'m done with the stimulus, we will talk through what each step was, why it was there, how it worked, all that kind of good stuff.\\n- 0:37And so let\'s give it a try. All right. So in its coverage of a controversy regarding a proposal to build a new freeway, a television news program showed interviews with several people who would be affected by the proposed freeway.\\n- 0:48Okay. So we have this TV news program and it\'s covering this controversy, around a proposed new freeway in a given community.\\n- 0:55And they\'re interviewing people who would be affected by this new freeway. And it\'s like, oh, you know, a bunch of people probably complaining about like noise, pollution and stuff.\\n- 1:01Right. I\'m like, oh, damn with the freeway, my property value. And so of the interviews shown, those conducted with people for it, two to one.\\n- 1:12Okay. So of the interviews they showed, then you saw that it was a two to one ratio of people who were against the freeway to people who were before, who were for the freeway.\\n- 1:18And it\'s like, honestly, I\'m surprised it was only that tilted. Because the people affected by freeways, like, I, you can be affected negatively by a freeway in a lot of ways, you know, that the people aren\'t really going to be out there, you know, love the freeway.\\n- 1:32Like, that\'s not like really a thing. So the television news, the television program is therefore based biased against the proposed freeway.\\n- 1:38Okay. So the TV news program is biased against the freeway and it\'s like, or they\'re just showing an adequate representation of the sample of people affected by the that a two to one ratio of against freeway to for freeway is actually kind of rosy.\\n- 1:53In my opinion, this like, sounds like a pretty popular freeway because like people who are mad about noise pollution make a lot more noise and want to be on TV news way more than people who are like, my commute will be better, whatever.\\n- 2:03Okay. And so what you just saw there, was a deconstructed version of that stimulus. And so as you saw, I read the first sentence and then I looked away, right?\\n- 2:12Because you want to look away when you\'re translating, just to make sure that you don\'t reread the text while you\'re translating.\\n- 2:17It\'s really important that the rereading and the translating don\'t get mixed up. And so when you saw me reread or not Weird, ugh.\\n- 2:24When you saw me look away as I was doing it, I was doing my Translation. And then when you saw me start to bring some of my own investigation, my own context, where I was like, oh, you know, noise pollution, my property value.\\n- 2:37That\'s the quipping part. When I was saying on the Two Point Ratio that, okay, that sounds pretty good. I\'m surprised they got that many people who were for the freeway.\\n- 2:45That moment of surprise, that\'s quipping, right? That\'s when I\'m bringing my own energy, my own ideas, and I\'m taking apart the stimulus and looking at it with a little bit of curiosity and investigation, right?\\n- 2:56And so then, one thing you might\'ve noticed is how that quipping really led into that CLIR that I had at the end of the stimulus, right?\\n- 3:03And if you don\'t know what the CLIR is yet, cause you haven\'t got to that part in The Loophole book, totally fine.\\n- 3:08Just bear with us. The CLIR is an analytical output you have at the end of every stimulus. But yeah, that\'s if you notice my CLIR there, it kind of bled into my final quip on the stimulus, right?\\n- 3:17That, that last sentence though, you know, they\'re biased against the freeway. I translated it. Okay. TV news is biased against the freeway.\\n- 3:24They don\'t like this freeway. And then I was like, or it\'s an adequate representation of the sample that, or it\'s an adequate representation of the sample moment.\\n- 3:32That was my last quip, but it also really functions as a CLIR. And that\'s because the CLIR works as kind of a subset of quipping, which is kind of cool, right?\\n- 3:42That the CLIR is a certain type of analytical takeaway and quipping is all your analytical takeaways that you could have at various moments in response to, in response to pieces of text in the stimulus.\\n- 3:53And so when you get to that end of the stimulus, that last quip very naturally becomes your CLIR because the CLIR is just the most appropriate analytical takeaway to have.\\n- 4:01At the end of the stimulus. And that fits perfectly in your quip slot. And so, as you can see, this is a very like fluid thinking process of reacting to the text as you\'re going, making sure that you have it deeply within your brain and doing a nice read where you\'re making sure that information gets\\n- 4:15Straight into the old brain organism. All right. I love you. I hope that this helped and we\'ll dive deep into each stage of the RTT process as we go forward next.\\n\\n##### Day 2\\n\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\n\\n**PAUSE!**\\n\\nGo to **PT135.S1.Q5** in LawHub\\\\* so you can see the question as I translate it. After you\'ve tried the question yourself, watch the whole video to compare my process with yours.\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"Whats Rtq • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/whats-rtq?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-24\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## What\'s RTQ?\\n\\nIt is a process, an operation\\n\\n0:32\\n\\n/\\n\\n4:31\\n\\n- 0:00What is RTQ? I mean, I have been waiting so long to be able to tell you this. I\'m so excited. OK,\\n- 0:07so RTQ stands for read, translate, quip. And it\'s actually an acronym that one of my students came up with that I now look back and I\'m like, you know, they were right.\\n- 0:17That\'s a that\'s a good way to encapsulate it, because RTQ is basically a unit. You read and you translate and you quip on every piece of text that you enCounter throughout a stimulus, an answer choice, a question to them, a passage, whatever it might be. It is a process, an operation that you perform\\n- 0:34on chunks of text as you go. And so this is something you would be doing instead of writing down the conceptual information in your Translation like we have in the Loophole book.\\n- 0:43It\'s totally fine to start with that writing down of conceptual information as a practice. You begin when you\'re reading a Loophole book.\\n- 0:49It\'s not harmful. It\'s good. It\'s kind of a good like warm up to be able to get us into the RTQ process.\\n- 0:56And the RTQ process is just a little bit more actively engaging throughout the stimulus as opposed to saving it all until the end, which is, you know, interesting because it allows us to do more things.\\n- 1:05And so let me explain just the basics of how it works, and then we\'ll have in-depth videos that go through each step for you next.\\n- 1:11And so RTQ, essentially, let\'s say a sentence is a nice full idea chunk. And that that doesn\'t necessarily mean that you would stop to RTQ at the end of a sentence only. Maybe you have like a comma and you want to have a sentence.\\n- 1:25Maybe it\'s actually, you know, more like breaking it into thirds of a sentence. Typically, I tell people don\'t RTQ combining sentences unless you\'re, you know, very advanced and you feel very, very confident that you absolutely will retain all the conceptual details of everything you\'ve read.\\n- 1:42Because usually when someone combines two sentences, especially when they\'re in the beginner stages, the first sentence\'s details will often be lost. And we don\'t want that to happen.\\n- 1:51And so I often say, try and figure out where you want to break in your read to RTQ. By thinking of texts like this coffee cup, noticing\\n- 1:57all my videos, I always have a coffee cup as a prop. It\'s because they\'re like five on this desk.\\n- 2:02So as you\'re reading, you want to think of words as filling the coffee cup. And the words should go until about here, not here. When you stop to translate,\\n- 2:13meaning you don\'t want to be retaking on so much text at a time that the words are starting to get lost and overflow.\\n- 2:19You want to be a little bit more conservative in your break strategy of like I read, read, read, read, read until I\'m to about here in terms of the amount of words I\'ve taken it. And I\'m like, OK, I know I\'ve got this. I know it\'s not going to spill. I can stop to RTQ.\\n- 2:33And so that would be when you stop, like I said, rounding down instead of rounding up, because if you round up, the chances of lost information are much higher than if you round down.\\n- 2:42Some exceptions being, you know, if you really don\'t have a full idea of what\'s happened yet, you know, that you don\'t have any complete thought. OK, you can take on a little more text. But like I said, be really careful about going beyond one sentence at a time, especially to start.\\n- 2:56But that read stage of the RTQ process, that\'s the first stage. You want to make sure that you\'re fully focusing on your read while you\'re doing it, that you\'re not mixing in other steps, especially in the beginning.\\n- 3:08You want to have full focus on just taking in kind of like a one way street on a road that\'s like we\'re just bringing information into the brain in that read stage. And then the Translation stage is when we say back to ourselves the conceptual information, what we just read. And we\'re going\\n- 3:24to say it in our own words, like we\'re telling a story to a friend. And then in our quip stage, this is the new stuff. This is when we react to what we just read.\\n- 3:34We show curiosity, we show skepticism, we have an independent thought about what we just read, bringing ourselves into an active alignment with the author of the stimulus, having a little bit of a back and forth.\\n- 3:45And that quip stage is super, super, super important to do after every every read read and translate stage, because it keeps you really conceptually alive throughout the stimulus, really active so that you\'re taking the question apart. And\\n- 4:01essentially the quips are where you\'re getting the question right. And so that\'s why it\'s really important because you\'re actively taking the stimulus apart.\\n- 4:08This is when you\'re essentially doing your analytical thinking to enable you to do the work to eventually arrive at the correct answer to the question.\\n- 4:15It\'s kind of like your margin notes on the stimulus as you are going. And so that\'s the basics of RTQ.\\n- 4:22I hope that that helped. We\'re just going to go into a lot more of the details on every individual stage in these next couple of videos.\\n- 4:28So definitely stay tuned. All right. I\'ll see you soon.\\n\\n##### Day 2\\n\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\n\\nAnd this one is for those who justly complain on Reddit that I already have too many acronyms in *The Loophole* 😘\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"Whats the Translation Standard Were Trying to Hit • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/whats-the-translation-standard-were-trying-to-hit?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## What\'s the Translation Standard We\'re Trying to Hit?\\n\\n1:23\\n\\n/\\n\\n2:36\\n\\n- 0:00What is the Translation standard we\'re trying to hit? You know, what does good look like? What is the Elysium at the end of the rainbow here?\\n- 0:07It is that when you read and translate a stimulus, you have a 100% conceptual awareness, 100% conceptual retention of all of the information that is presented to you in that stimulus.\\n- 0:19Now, that does not mean that you are literally reciting every word of the stimulus in the exact order it\'s given absolutely verbatim.\\n- 0:27You\'re totally free to translate into your own words using your own language and also to take things out of order.\\n- 0:35But when I say translate things in your own words, I don\'t mean take away the details. What I mean is save the details in your own words.\\n- 0:44And maybe that\'s fewer words because you\'re able to conceptualize the information in a more efficient way, but it actually covers all of the information.\\n- 0:52If that\'s so, that\'s totally fine. But I don\'t want you to take efficiency in your Translation as an excuse to leave information out\\n- 0:59because you need all the concepts in the stimulus to be retained and presented within your Translation. And you wanna retain your entire Translation as you\'re going into the answer choice because you need that information,\\n- 1:10that information interrelated with the information that\'s given to you in the answer choices is how you\'ll know what is correct and what is incorrect.\\n- 1:17But the standard isn\'t just conceptual retention and conceptual awareness, it\'s also the way in which that conceptual awareness is gained.\\n- 1:25Eventually what you want is for Translation and quipping to feel like breathing, feel like water, feel like something that you cannot turn off.\\n- 1:33It\'s just when you read, you are naturally translating without having to volitionally tell yourself that you have to translate. And without having to say, okay, I need to remember this, look for this, remember that, you just do remember it. That is the goal of mastery that we\'re\\n- 1:51hoping to achieve here. When I am going through and reading something, the reason why I didn\'t know Translation existed for so long is because I did not know that I was actually doing all of this.\\n- 2:00It was just that at the end of reading a stimulus, I\'d of course be able to tell you everything that happened because I thought that was just the way people read.\\n- 2:08That\'s what we want for you is that as you\'re going through, all of these things are naturally happening in a subconscious way that form a great process that at the end of the stimulus, you\'re like, yeah, of course I know what it said.\\n- 2:18How could I not? Of course I know what it said. How could I not? Isn\'t that just such a gift of entitlement?\\n- 2:24Of course I know what it said. How could I not? That\'s the standard that we\'re going for at the end of every stimulus in logical reasoning at the end of every passage as well.\\n- 2:35All right, I\'ll see you soon.\\n\\n##### Day 5\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"What Is Reading You Are Not Broken • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/what-is-reading-you-are-not-broken?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-24\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## What is Reading? (you are not broken)\\n\\nor they will just not have any sub vocalization at all.\\n\\n1:16\\n\\n/\\n\\n6:29\\n\\n- 0:00What is reading and what should it be? I know I\'m probably asking a very annoying reductive question right now But I swear I have a more novel than average answer\\n- 0:08When I ask what is reading what I\'m asking is what is it for you when you read? What are you really doing?\\n- 0:18And what are your intentions when you are actually doing that act of reading? Like what do you really want to have happen?\\n- 0:25Because very often and when people read they are not necessarily reading to get all the nuances of every single thing that is on the page right\\n- 0:35because there are lots of different intentions that somebody might have when they\'re reading But very very often what you will find is what people call reading is more like some of the following\\n- 0:46I listed out just a couple of habits that I have observed over the years that people do when they read\\n- 0:50they will take on Multiple sentences all at once that they\'re just kind of like looking at them overall. They\'ll scan for proper nouns.\\n- 0:57They\'ll omit connector words They\'ll get the gist. They\'ll omit the details. They\'ll combine the lists into those things They\'ll say okay, you know, and this is something about coins or this is something about boats.\\n- 1:08This is something about light bulbs they\'ll Kind of say the words in their head But it will be in a monotone or they will just not have any sub vocalization at all Vocalization\\n- 1:18means the voice in your head and they will often skip words that they don\'t want to pronounce And so if a word is long, they will just skip it.\\n- 1:27They won\'t actually even really read it They\'ll skip names. They\'ll skip dates. They\'ll skip numbers. They\'ll skip anything that makes them uncomfortable\\n- 1:36they just won\'t actually read it and This problem I call it a problem because I don\'t really consider this to be a reading for depth Sort of protocol that we could follow follows us into the LSAT\\n- 1:50and it causes a lot of Heartache because all of those things that are you know safe ish to do in real life are Super not safe to do on the LSAT when you actually have to recall all the idiosyncrasies of what you\'re reading\\n- 2:06And you actually do have to recall the idiosyncrasies while you\'re doing the LSAT The issue is the LSAT requires so much more of us than what these reading habits are designed to give us that There\'s just a misalignment there.\\n- 2:20And so just for the purposes of our conversation because this is a conversation to me. It\'s a conversation Let\'s call all those strategies, you know of like it\'s about coins and skipping the stuff that\'s confusing to me Let\'s call that ghost reading\\n- 2:34ghost reading is how I like to think of it in my in my head and It\'s a reading strategy that\'s designed for like a Verizon bill You just got in the mail where it\'s like I don\'t really want to read this thing\\n- 2:46I\'m just trying to figure out about whatever, you know new fee you\'re putting out on my bill and like do I have to cancel\\n- 2:52and it\'s a style of reading that is designed for really one thing which is speed to being done and To get done with the act of reading faster\\n- 3:03And so when you think about that think about it for a second that like our our goal when we\'re reading is to not read\\n- 3:10That isn\'t exactly a goal that is you know aligned for Retaining all the nuances of the details of the complexity of the information and all that\\n- 3:22you were literally telling your brain I want to not do what I\'m doing while I\'m doing it I mean, that\'s that\'s some silliness\\n- 3:28and for those of you out there who are like, oh no, Ellen I love to read I want to read\\n- 3:32I want to you know, like marinate in the Tea of information that is going on. I love you\\n- 3:40and thank you and you\'re in a world where this happens But one thing I really want to tell you is if you\'re doing any of the things that I just described and we all do Them when we are reading Verizon bills,\\n- 3:51so this is no moral judgment on my behalf think of me like a researcher I\'m someone who\'s trying to understand the situation here\\n- 3:57But if you are doing those things, which I think you do at least sometimes, you know When you\'re looking at a Verizon bill, I just want to tell you The fact that sometimes when you\'re doing the LSAT you have trouble remembering what you read trouble understanding what you read\\n- 4:11It doesn\'t mean you\'re broken You\'re not broken. You\'re just bringing a bad tool to the wrong fight You\'ve been socialized and trained to look at words a certain way and do certain activities.\\n- 4:24That\'s fine Like I said, no moral judgment here But those tools that you\'ve been socialized to use, you know what\\n- 4:31they just don\'t work that well over here and That doesn\'t mean that you\'re stupid. It doesn\'t mean that you\'re dumb\\n- 4:39you\'re not dumb and This is something that\'s just really really important to me to let you know, you do not have any kind of disorder Necessarily,\\n- 4:49you don\'t have a learning disability necessarily and for those of our friends who do you know more power to you?\\n- 4:56Love you, too But just the fact that you are having trouble remembering what you read it doesn\'t necessarily mean that there is any sort of significant Biological issue going on.\\n- 5:08It\'s just that you know, you\'re you\'re trying to I don\'t know Speed through a residential street when you know That\'s not really what the signs are built for and you might get a ticket\\n- 5:18Like that is just a habit that you have been built to have but it does not mean that you can\'t make another habit\\n- 5:25Right now when one is ghost reading essentially you\'re just treating a skill that is like one of those lifelong mastery sorts of skills like it\'s a utility like it\'s something Disposable\\n- 5:37but we can treat reading like Japanese masters treat the making of you know knives and swords and things like they treat Making sushi like any artisan treats their craft the thing that they love to do day in and day out And they get better and better and better at it over time Reading thrives with\\n- 5:56that sort of treatment and we can treat it like that We don\'t have to treat it as something that our only intention while we do it is to be done with it Faster\\n- 6:06and when you switch this mindset and you start wanting to marinate in the tea of the words or maybe that tisane A tisane might be better.\\n- 6:13Um, I think you\'ll see a big big difference But as you get into these later videos I really want to remind you take your time and let yourself marinate in the tisane\\n- 6:24speed is not the only thing that matters All right.
