Well, because I\'ve been doing a lot of stuff since the Loophole came out.\\n- 0:07I\'ve probably taught the LSAT for over 10,000 hours. And like I actually did the math. It\'s not probably. I have.\\n- 0:14Since the Loophole was published. The Loophole was published at the very end of 2018. And so it hasn\'t actually been that long. I just love what I do.\\n- 0:23I do LSAT a lot all the time, seven days a week. But when the Loophole book was published, it was in 2018. And I discovered Translation overall in around 2015. So I had about three years of working on Translation to finalize the version that you see in the Loophole book, which I still\\n- 0:42endorse. You know, this is not me saying that don\'t do the Translation just in the Loophole book.\\n- 0:47It definitely still has a place in curriculum that I will get to. But I want you to know just that this Methodology of Translation, it has been evolving for literally 10 years at this point.\\n- 0:57And what you see in the Loophole book is the Loophole Translation Method about three years in ish. We\'re in 2025 now, but it\'s beginning of 2025. So that\'s six and a half or whatever years in.\\n- 1:10And we\'ve had a lot of evolution since then. My tutoring students up until the Loophole book were published were absolute wonderful advocates who helped me understand how Translation worked and got me to the place where I could come up with what\'s in the Loophole book.\\n- 1:22It works and it\'s great. But I have been working since with even more tutoring students to get a more expanded universe of what Translation can mean.\\n- 1:30And so the Translation Drill that you do in the Loophole book where you write down the Translations on each stimuli,\\n- 1:36it\'s a wonderful drill to have really high levels of accountability for your own self checks between your self, like writing something down and the actual Translation of the stimulus.\\n- 1:47Because it\'s a written product, it enables you to actually check really well whether you got everything in the stimulus.\\n- 1:54It lets you know that you\'re right. And so part of the reason why that was the version of Translation that went into the Loophole book\\n- 2:00is because I knew that I wasn\'t going to necessarily be able to have a conversation, have more of a like human interaction with every single person who Read The Loophole book.\\n- 2:10Even though through the book, I feel like I am. But I wasn\'t going to be able to have a back and forth conversation to be able to tell how good their Translations were.\\n- 2:19So I needed a process that was going to be able to be checkable by the person doing it. And that\'s a lot of why that written process exists.\\n- 2:27I mean, it also was just the first thing I tried and it worked well enough that I liked it.\\n- 2:32But then after the Loophole book came out, I got a lot more tutoring students and I was you know, there were times in 2019 and 2020 where I was tutoring like. 60 to 70 hours a week, maybe even over 70 hours a week.\\n- 2:47There were times I was pulling like 14 hour days, like not infrequently. I was not somebody who really knew how to run a business.\\n- 2:55I didn\'t really know what I was doing at all. But in that time period of 2019 and 2020, I enCountered some of the pitfalls of Translation when it was written.\\n- 3:06Once you got past to it, like a more advanced level, because as I wanted to up level people even further, I was noticing that there were ways that when people would do it out loud with me in session instead of writing. If they didn\'t have the more expanded universe that you\'re going to\\n- 3:24learn about here, which is intonation and quipping and all that different kind of stuff. What I would notice is sometimes they would say the words or write the words, but I could tell they didn\'t really know what the words signify.\\n- 3:36They didn\'t really understand the deeper meaning and deeper connections between the ideas that the words entailed.\\n- 3:43And so I began experimenting with lots of different things and seeing what worked across all of the wonderful body of tutoring students that I had at the time.\\n- 3:52And like I said, I was tutoring a lot until I had a lot of students that I could very rapidly iterate and test what I was working on in a way that I couldn\'t do before the Loophole book came out.\\n- 4:03And, you know, I had way, way fewer students just because, you know, some chicken palo alto who loved the LSAT. Like people didn\'t really know who I was at all.\\n- 4:10But then all of a sudden, after the Loophole came out, you know, I had way more people who wanted to study with me\\n- 4:14and way more people were really like down and wanted to be the test subject. And so I was able to figure out intonation and quipping in 2019.\\n- 4:25And since then, I have been iterating on that, what I call the RTQ process. It stands for read, translate, quip.\\n- 4:33I\'ve been iterating on the RTQ process since 2019, really dialing it in to make sure that it works the way it should work as a next phase of the Translation process that\'s in the Loophole book.\\n- 4:45Because here in, you know, Loophole Online land, I actually, you know, I feel like I can have a little bit more of a full relationship with you all where there are more resources to be able to actually like see how good you are in a way that the disconnected, you know, feeling of a book where\\n- 5:02it\'s, you know, it\'s not video. It\'s not it doesn\'t it doesn\'t feel as alive, you know, even though I do endeavor to make the Loophole book be as alive as it possibly can.\\n- 5:10But in this online platform, I\'m a little bit closer to you. So I feel like I can give you a process that it\'s not as, you know, easily checkable. Maybe now in the world of AI, where you can have AI transcribe your Translations for you, you actually can check your Translations a little bit easier\\n- 5:27than you could have maybe 2018. But in this world, I feel I finally have like the resources and the team and the platform to bring you what I have always wanted to bring you for the past six years.\\n- 5:41You do not know how important doing this has been to me to finally, finally be able to share with my Loophole book audience that I love so much. The things that I\'ve been working on with so, so few people\\n- 5:58and, you know, you have to work with few people to really understand whether what you\'re doing works because you need that really in-depth ability to see, you know, you have to study and you have to adjust and you have to dial it in to like make sure that it works. Because if\\n- 6:12it doesn\'t work, you know, it\'s trash. Get rid of it. But I am convinced because I\'ve had six years to make sure that this worked and it does work. And I have wanted to give it to you for so long. And it\'s been really, really hard to get this\\n- 6:25whole thing up and running. But it is now and it\'s going to be yours finally. And so thank you for being here with me and for actually, you know, listening to half this cockeyed theories I have about all this stuff of, you know, helping you learn how to really retain and work with information.\\n- 6:45And prove to you that you\'re a lot smarter than you really think you are. All right. I love you. Of course, I get emotional this video. All right.\\n- 6:52I\'ll see you soon.\\n\\n##### Day 2\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"Why Its Good to Be Hypersensitive • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/why-its-good-to-be-hypersensitive?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## Why It\'s Good to Be Hypersensitive\\n\\n0:06\\n\\n/\\n\\n5:44\\n\\n- 0:00why it\'s good to be hypersensitive. So this is coming from someone who is hypersensitive. And so absolutely no shade. Being hypersensitive is super, super useful in the LSAT.\\n- 0:10The reason why it\'s good is because it allows you to avert all kinds of bad consequences later on in a question or a section.\\n- 0:16Those bad consequences being wrong CLIRs, wrong answers, confusion, like time panic because you don\'t know the answer, all different kinds of stuff like that.\\n- 0:26And so what do I mean, though, when I say hypersensitive? Mostly what I mean is if you are reading something and there\'s like a word in what you\'re reading, you notice it and you find significance in that word that you\'re reading.\\n- 0:38People who are less sensitive are more likely to overlook certain words because they don\'t think they really matter that much.\\n- 0:45They don\'t think that they\'re very important. Like, I honestly can\'t tell you how many times I\'ve written in someone\'s tutoring notes, pay attention to the adjectives and adverbs in a stimulus or in an answer choice.\\n- 0:56And this isn\'t because adjectives and adverbs are more important than other words in the stimulus. Like I say, every word in the stimulus is the most important word in the stimulus.\\n- 1:03The reason why I write that so much is because people who are less sensitive tend to overlook adjectives and adverbs.\\n- 1:10And so everything being equal, that will be something that I correct more often simply because it\'s more likely to be overlooked.\\n- 1:17And the people who write the exam know that people who are less sensitive to individual words will tend to overlook an adjective or an adverb typically more often than they\'re going to overlook a noun or a verb.\\n- 1:28And so they\'ll often pack a ton of meaning, a ton of significance, a ton of influence over the rest of the stimulus into that adjective or adverb.\\n- 1:37And that will then cast a large shadow over the rest of the question that if you didn\'t get that adjective or adverb, getting the correct answer is going to be a much, much harder venture for you.\\n- 1:48In fact, this morning in tutoring, I wrote this note and it was because the person I was with, they did not see the word recent in recent findings.\\n- 1:59And there was another time duration early in the stimulus that they just auto applied to the next sentence instead of actually seeing the word recent and applying it to what the recent was talking about.\\n- 2:09And this is just the kind of very innocent sort of LSAT mistake that people will often write off as like, oh, just a misread.\\n- 2:16Oh, I just need to pay closer attention and all of that different kind of stuff. And they\'ll act like these sorts of mistakes aren\'t like that big of a deal. But these sorts of mistakes are actually like probably the majority of LSAT mistakes.\\n- 2:28And so any sorts of actions that we take to lessen our propensity toward those mistakes is really going to be in our benefit. Right.\\n- 2:36Because obviously, like if a given cause makes up the majority of mistakes, well, then you would want to go and make an impact on that set of mistakes just because there are so many. I don\'t\\n- 2:48want you to when you see a mistake like this, like, oh, I just didn\'t see recent. Oh, whatever. No, it\'s not whatever. It\'s I need to be more sensitive to what is going on in the stimulus.\\n- 3:00I need to not write off little things that I don\'t think matter just to go faster or because, you know, I just want the gist or whatever else. I need to really focus and be super sensitive to what I\'m doing.\\n- 3:09Very often, I will describe reading as like it\'s like a tactile sense and like Powerful words. They feel like cold metal, like super solid, Provable words.\\n- 3:18They feel like they\'re flowing like water. But it\'s almost like when you\'re reading, it\'s like Morse code or Braille a little bit where you can just you can feel the ups and downs of what you\'re reading.\\n- 3:27This also goes with your intonation. I know I sound unhinged when I talk about stuff like this, but that sensitivity of feeling those words in a deeper way, being super invested in what you\'re doing when you\'re doing it, it is the biggest effortless hack there is. And I know it might sound silly right\\n- 3:47now, but if you can try and invest more in your intonation and actually making every word pop just a little bit, you\'ll get closer and closer to feeling those words more directly and not having to think about how much adjectives matter.\\n- 4:01Because I don\'t want you sitting on the actual day of the test and being like, oh, Ellen told me I need to pay attention to the adjectives.\\n- 4:06So like, here I am. Look at the adjective. That\'s not what I want at all. I want you to uplift your general level of like care and obsession with every little word so that those adjectives are naturally retained.\\n- 4:17But one more story from this morning, the same student that I was with this morning, she had cut out a full dependent clause,\\n- 4:25what you what you might call an optional element from the first chapter of the Loophole. It was just it was like consequently and then words in a comma and then next part of sentence.\\n- 4:35And this dependent clause or optional element, it was specifying the group that the Conclusion was supposed to apply to. And she completely left it out of her Translation and completely left the information out of her CLIR.\\n- 4:47And then the CLIR was off because that information was actually pretty operative in terms of how you should be CLIRing the stimulus and how you would then go into the answer choice and what answer is right.\\n- 4:56And when I brought it up to her, she\'s like, oh, I just didn\'t think that was very important. And I bring this up to show you that you don\'t actually know what is important when you first meet a set of text.\\n- 5:07When you\'re first looking at the first letter of a dependent clause, you cannot know whether or not that is going to be operative in a later stage of the question or not.\\n- 5:17And so it pays to be sensitive to that information regardless of what you might be future projecting in terms of its importance, because you will be wrong as often as you\'re right in terms of your ability to predict that sort of behavior of the text.\\n- 5:32And so you want to just increase your overall level of sensitivity to the point where you can feel those words in your soul, too. All right.\\n- 5:40I love you. I hope you don\'t think I\'m too much of a weirdo and I will see you soon.\\n\\n##### Day 5\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"Why Outside Knowledge Is Not the Devil • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/why-outside-knowledge-is-not-the-devil?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## Why Outside Knowledge is Not the Devil\\n\\nand they, they maybe have pearls\\n\\n4:43\\n\\n/\\n\\n7:52\\n\\n- 0:00That\'s right. Outside knowledge isn\'t the devil. I know you want to come for me. It\'s okay. Outside knowledge is not the devil that people make it out to be in LSAT prep.\\n- 0:11So I want to tell you why and I want to tell you why people say it and why you shouldn\'t care.\\n- 0:15It is not the devil because it is unavoidable. When you read a word on the LSAT in logical reasoning or in reading comp, you have no choice but to know the definition of that word and to know the general context in which words like that appear.\\n- 0:32For instance, if you read the word cigarette, how do you know what a cigarette is? You know, because of outside knowledge, right?\\n- 0:40And when you read the word cigarette, I would say it\'s generally accepted to know that these are entities that people smoke and there\'s nicotine involved and there\'s health consequences perhaps associated and stuff like that.\\n- 0:53Like when we call this common knowledge about cigarettes, right? And a lot of times, I think when we talk about outside knowledge on the LSAT, we act like you\'re not allowed to know that cigarettes are often associated with health consequences, that that\'s like some kind of weird imaginary veil\\n- 1:10you have to place over your understanding. But that\'s impossible. That\'s not like a real thing that a person can do.\\n- 1:17When you read the word cigarettes, you\'re going to have to use your outside knowledge to Fill In the definition of that word.\\n- 1:24And at some point you might be like, oh, well, okay, well, I can know what cigarettes is, but, you know, I shouldn\'t assume that the cigarettes are what we smoke through the mouth and we shouldn\'t assume that there are perhaps health consequences associated with those cigarettes and\\n- 1:37all that different kind of stuff. But I would say you kind of don\'t have a choice because all of those associations are essentially intrinsic to the concept of a cigarette.\\n- 1:46And if you are narrowing yourself down in what you allow the definitions of words to be as you read them on the LSAT, so far that you\'re like, well, is it a smoking cigarette?\\n- 1:57I mean, you\'re essentially playing a losing game. You know, like there is a level of outside knowledge that is simply necessary to read words and perform analytical operations on this test\\n- 2:08to question logical relationships. You have to have some level of knowledge of what the concepts being explored are. And I find very often that this aversion to outside knowledge because of previous cautions that people have heard in the preposphere makes people very afraid to quit because they\'re\\n- 2:24like, well, I\'m not allowed to say anything outside the text and all of this kind of stuff. And it\'s like, girl, you\'re allowed to have thoughts and beliefs and use your beautiful brain.\\n- 2:35You know, like this is, I think, one of the ways that accidentally in prep we make smart people feel worse than they actually should is we tell people they\'re not allowed to use all of the wonderful associations that they\'ve made throughout their life between different sorts of ideas.\\n- 2:49And I just, you know, I complain about being people being skeptical of me, but I actually am the ultimate not follow the rules type of person.\\n- 2:56And when I was prepping, I didn\'t really care what anyone said about outside knowledge. I was like, well, I\'m going to read it the way I read it\\n- 3:02and I\'m going to think about it the way I think about it. So whatever. And I just would go through and question whatever I wanted to question using whatever knowledge I wanted to use, because\\n- 3:09I was like, girl, bye. I mean, like, what am I going to do? How am I going to know what kind of knowledge is good knowledge and bad knowledge?\\n- 3:15And this becomes like some kind of random epistemological question that we\'re juggling in the middle of taking a 35 minute L.R. section of like, oh, is this knowledge common enough of what the definition of a cigarette is\\n- 3:26or is this knowledge too esoteric? And all of that is a question that doesn\'t have an answer. There is no line we can draw in the sand of this kind of knowledge is good\\n- 3:35and that kind of knowledge is bad. And it would be an impossible question to answer even if it did have an answer in the context of a 35 minute section.\\n- 3:42And so why am I telling you all of this? I\'m telling you all of this because I want you to start using your beautiful brain.\\n- 3:47I want you to actually bring in all the beautiful knowledge and context of the world that you have as a thinking, breathing human who is existing in the world and start to use your common sense from real life, because your common sense is what informs reasonable logical relationships versus non reasonable\\n- 4:04logical relationships. And I want you to start getting questions right on that basis. Now, let me tell you why this is all come to be. And this is like the whole guarding against outside knowledge thing is such a prevalent prescription in LSAT prep.\\n- 4:18Well, people tell you to be afraid of outside knowledge because the LSAT is not a knowledge test. The LSAT is not grading\\n- 4:25you on how much you know about oysters coming into the test. Right. It\'s not as though you should have been studying up on what waters they like to live in or whatever. But the LSAT does expect you that when you read oysters, you know, you know, these are sea creatures that\\n- 4:40they live in, you know, water and they maybe have pearls and they\'re bivalves. Maybe not bivalves. That was probably a little bit of high school bio talking.\\n- 4:47But that they expect you to know those sorts of general things, but they\'re not testing you on how many species of oyster are there.\\n- 4:55You don\'t have to know that as some kind of preexisting fact. But of course, you have to be attuned if the stimulus brings you that information.\\n- 5:00So, you know, bully for all of us, but you need the context of an oyster to apply that how many species there may be information that may come to you in the stimulus.\\n- 5:08So since the test is not devoted to testing your preexisting knowledge of oysters and whatnot, people are like, don\'t bring that in.\\n- 5:15And I can only guess that this is like some kind of lowest common denominator piece of advice of like, don\'t choose an answer because, like, you know, in real life, there\'s this many species of oyster or my mom really likes the color blue. So blue is the best.\\n- 5:29And I pick B because it says blue or something like I don\'t really know, because in all my years of teaching and this is thousands and thousands of hours of one on one else that prep, I can probably count on one hand the number of times I\'ve ever seen anyone miss a question due to misusing outside knowledge.\\n- 5:46And 100 percent of those times were because someone was an expert in the field they were doing the question on, as in somebody had a Ph.D. In like, what was it like? Neuro engineering or something\\n- 5:59was something with the brain and machines and they were doing an R.C. passage that was something related to the brain.\\n- 6:05And so when people have that kind of outside expertise, it\'s very challenging for them to understand what is common knowledge and what is not.\\n- 6:13If you are not coming into this as some kind of world expert in a topic that you are about to get questions on. Hey, congrats.\\n- 6:20But when you get questions related to, say, your major or things like that, remember, you\'re not being expected here to, like, reinvent what supply and demand should best be calculated as.\\n- 6:34You\'re just expected to be able to navigate concepts of supply and demand using basic tools. But you\'re not going to have to do any sort of unreal calculations or anything like that.\\n- 6:44That\'s totally fine. And so I don\'t want you to be disempowered in any way, thinking that, like, oh, because outside knowledge is good, I have to, like, become an expert in all these various fields.\\n- 6:55No. But as you probably can see from looking at the curriculum, we do want to boost your amount of common knowledge that you\'re bringing to the exam.\\n- 7:03For instance, if you\'ve ever heard of trade secrets before you do R.C., you probably have a leg up. But ever hearing of trade secrets and I\'m going to write a treatise on trade secrets are two very different things.\\n- 7:13We want you more on the former side of the line rather than the latter. And so I hope that this helps that you do not have to be afraid of bringing your real experience, your real knowledge and like how you exist in the world and think of relationships as reasonable.\\n- 7:28You don\'t have to be afraid of bringing that into the exam. This is a demon that doesn\'t really exist. It\'s kind of like a paper tiger that\'s been threatening you all along.\\n- 7:37And if you do get a question wrong because of outside knowledge, shoot me an email. Now there, I am really putting my money where my mouth is with this, right?\\n- 7:46Because am I about to have my inbox blow up? I don\'t think I will. All right. I\'ll see you soon.\\n\\n##### Day 5\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"8c209a","wall_time_seconds":0.2013479,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":5871,"output":"---\\ntitle: \\"Take a Guess at Words You Dont Know • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/take-a-guess-at-words-you-dont-know?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## Take a Guess at Words You Don\'t Know\\n\\n0:14\\n\\n/\\n\\n6:58\\n\\n- 0:00Take a guess at words you don\'t know. This is so important because I\'m going to outline for you a problem that I see a lot when people go through translating and they get to a word that they do not know.\\n- 0:11When people are reading and they see a word they don\'t know, very often what they will do is just glaze over that word and keep on going. And they will then allow the not knowing of that one word to morph into an ever-expanding black hole that surrounds the surrounding ideas.\\n- 0:31And now there\'s a big black hole in the middle of the sentence and the stuff on the edge of the word you don\'t know is now being encompassed by it as like, oh, well, I just don\'t know what that means.\\n- 0:39I\'m, and I don\'t know that word, so I\'m not going to know what it means. And what they want to do is keep on reading, just blowing past this word they don\'t know, and hoping that later on the rest of the text will help me understand what that word meant.\\n- 0:53So let me tell you why this doesn\'t work and why it\'s harmful. Because when you allow that word you don\'t know to permeate and become this big black hole that like, oh, I didn\'t know that word, so now I don\'t know anything really surrounding it either, there tends to be an avoidant mentality\\n- 1:09that accompanies that. That people generally feel uncomfortable acknowledging that there is a word they don\'t know in the moment and so they don\'t really want to sit in that kind of icky, uncomfortable feeling and so they want to move forward. They want to get away from it.\\n- 1:23And they tell themselves this little white lie of, oh, I\'m keeping on going because I don\'t have time to know what this means and reading more, maybe it\'ll help me know what it means.\\n- 1:32The truth of the matter is you do not actually go back to that word and figure out what it means.\\n- 1:36You just continue on and try and pretend that it isn\'t there. And the later paragraphs or whatever it is, they do not actually tell you what the word meant and you shouldn\'t pretend that actually going on is really in service of knowing what the word meant because it isn\'t\\n- 1:54actually true. That then there is this opening in the passage where, and of course this could be a stimulus too, so even though I\'m saying passage, this also applies to LR, but there\'s this opening and then if an answer choice is playing on anything associated with that opening, it\'s\\n- 2:10a big gap that can potentially create wrong answers. So let me tell you what to do instead. If you do get to a word that you do not know, I want that to instead of creating an avoidant mentality, I want it to create a mentality of attraction, curiosity, fixation,\\n- 2:24that when you see that word, you\'re like, hmm, I\'m not sure about that. OK, you\'re allowed to read like to the end of the sentence, the end of the thought for sure and then but when you get to the end of that sentence, I want you to go back and I want you to take an educated guess on what that\\n- 2:37word means. You cannot leave it open as just a blank. This is the biggest mistake that people make. And so, OK, I\'ve read the sentence, I go and I take a guess.\\n- 2:46Now, how often would you surmise that that guess that people take is actually correct? Because I will tell you in my observations of many, many students over many, many years, it\'s right probably like 90 plus percent of the time.\\n- 3:00When people take a guess, they\'re almost always correct. And of course, if they are correct and they run with that guess, wonderful.\\n- 3:09If they\'re incorrect and they run with their guess, OK, they have an inaccuracy in terms of what this word meant. Maybe it\'s a lot bit off, but they are much more likely to find success with the wrong understanding of a single word, but the correct\\n- 3:22understanding of surrounding words and no avoidance of the information in that overall sentence. They\'re much, much better off in terms of their ability to get a question right. That may follow that is either based or not based on that information.\\n- 3:34And so it is better, even if you guess wrong, to buy into your guess and use it moving forward because it\'s not enough to simply take a guess at the word you don\'t know. You then have to use that guess as though it is right and lock in on that meaning and use it going forward in your answering\\n- 3:54of the question. I know this might be challenging, but challenge yourself to do it because it actually really does yield return. But this also leans into our idea of we want to create a curiosity about things we don\'t understand rather than avoidance, right?\\n- 4:07Curiosity not avoidance. So one more thing about these words you don\'t know and how you are going to use them after the section is over.\\n- 4:13So be sure that when you\'re in section and you come across a word you don\'t know like this that you highlight it.\\n- 4:19And then at the end of the section, I want you to go back and harvest all of those highlighted words into your\\n- 4:25vocab tracker so you can build your customized vocab list and actually start to drill these words. I know that this is like the really unsexy part of LSAT prep where it\'s like, oh, well, Ellen, you know, who\'s even going to say that that word\'s going to come up again?\\n- 4:38Yeah, but who\'s going to say it doesn\'t? And I don\'t think it\'s really that big of a deal to harvest a word and put it in a vocab tracker and drill those words.\\n- 4:48It\'s going to help you in law school. It\'s going to help you in your general life to expand your vocabulary overall.\\n- 4:52Like you get the benefit of just having a better lexicon that aids you in your thinking, writing, reading all throughout your life.\\n- 5:00Like this is all just value add. So even if the word doesn\'t come up on the LSAT, you are better served for knowing it.\\n- 5:05But also, like we want to generally approach this with the mindset of expansiveness, you know, that we don\'t want to do the least\\n- 5:13we want to do the most. And so the fact that there even is a chance that this word would come up on a future exam is enough to cover that base\\n- 5:19because it\'s not that expensive to go and put the word in a vocab tracker and then go on to drill that vocab. And of course, I have other videos about all the idiosyncrasies of how to learn vocab with AI, not with AI, all that different kind of stuff.\\n- 5:31And so definitely avail yourself of those videos as well. I just want to point out that when you don\'t know what a word means, that is a flag telling you this is a place where I can be better.\\n- 5:40I need to study this. And there are so few parts of the LSAT that really like directly benefit from pure memorization.\\n- 5:48You know, there\'s things like conditional indicators, premise, Conclusion indicators, question stems, all that kind of stuff. And that\'s like wonderful. And you should memorize that stuff.\\n- 5:53But all of the vocab that you might not initially understand, that is an opportunity for you as well to just through pure memorizing the kind of stuff you would do on a normal exam to actually uplift your score, which is really fantastic.\\n- 6:05And also, you don\'t have to get this exact word again in order for it to raise your score next time,\\n- 6:12because if you learn this one word that you didn\'t know really well, you can then break it down into its component roots and start to memorize similarities and differences of let\'s say this word is presuppose\\n- 6:22and like you maybe you didn\'t you didn\'t know pre exist either. But presuppose, you know, is to suppose something before it happens.\\n- 6:30And now you know that pre is a prefix that means before and then you see pre exist next time. You\'re like, OK, I know what exists means.\\n- 6:37I know pre exist is not that word of a word. So don\'t come for me.\\n- 6:41But you can break those words apart and actually get a lot of benefit out of them, even on associated root words as well. And\\n- 6:49so I hope that this helps. Be patient with yourself. It is OK to expand your vocab in this process.\\n- 6:54And I look forward to your happy guessing. All right. See you soon.\\n\\n##### Day 4\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"Translate Prove You Were Listening • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/translate-prove-you-were-listening?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## Translate: Prove You Were Listening\\n\\n0:10\\n\\n/\\n\\n6:17\\n\\n- 0:00Translation, right? This is the bread and butter of the whole thing. And so, alright, how do you translate? Well, we\'ve just gotten out of our read stage, right?\\n- 0:10We\'ve just read, we\'ve beautifully intoned. Okay, great. And now one key thing, you are not going to read during your Translation stage, right?\\n- 0:18Because in the read stage, you\'re only reading. In the Translation stage, you\'re only translating. You will not be rereading during this stage.\\n- 0:24And so in order to keep yourself honest of actually, like, really not rereading, we\'re going to need you to either close your eyes or look away from the stimulus while you\'re translating, just so that we can build some level of accountability to not rereading during the Translation process.\\n- 0:38And so, like, why is it so important to not reread? Because if you\'re rereading during the Translation process, it\'s kind of like one way street, but two way cars.\\n- 0:46Things get jammed. And essentially, you aren\'t translating. You aren\'t really using your short term memory to engage with what you had just read.\\n- 0:53Instead, you\'re just really reading words again and kind of reading out loud again. But it\'s all jammed and messed up.\\n- 1:00And so it messes up the Translation stage. And it also makes you translate in their exact words way more often than is optimal.\\n- 1:05Because in the Translation stage, you really want to translate in your own words. You want to tell your own story.\\n- 1:11And that means that you are allowed to say the ideas out of order. You\'re allowed to say them in a more casual way.\\n- 1:17You\'re allowed to laugh while you\'re doing it. It\'s meant to be your own imbibed version of the text.\\n- 1:25And, you know, I love this one saying, I think one of my students told me this once, that their definition of intelligence is the ability to say complex things in a simple way.\\n- 1:34And I was like, oh, you\'re so smart. But I really think that is the essence of the Translation process as well.\\n- 1:42Because when you\'re translating text on the else, that very often that text is going to be quite gnarly, snarly, or early. You know, it\'s going to be all messed up.\\n- 1:50There\'s going to be a lot of kind of fancy words in it. And your job is to dress it down to size a little bit, because when you translate, you\'re saying it in your own words, in your own language, like you\'re telling a story to a friend.\\n- 2:02And that probably is not going to necessarily use all the exact same fancy sentence structures, all the exact same five dollar words, because you\'re making it your own.\\n- 2:12You\'re taking a complex set of complex text and you\'re making it simple. But one really key thing in making it simple, that\'s not the same thing as cutting out information. Because you really need to make sure that you have all of the conceptual details of the text present in your Translation.\\n- 2:31And what does that mean? It means that we need to remove your filter. We we don\'t just translate the gist.\\n- 2:37We don\'t just translate the summary. We don\'t just translate the important parts. All of that stuff is impossible nonsense, because here here\'s the big secret.\\n- 2:46It\'s impossible to know what the important parts of a piece of text are before you\'ve read it. Right. And really, that read stages when you\'re bringing the information in.\\n- 2:55And so if you\'re going to filter stuff out, like it would have to be, you know, not really coming in the door when it\'s first coming in.\\n- 3:02And then in the Translation stage, the reason you wouldn\'t translate it is because it\'s not there to translate. You want to translate all of the information that you have in your brain.\\n- 3:09You don\'t want to efficientize it and, you know, get rid of various stuff because that\'s not a Translation. That\'s something else.\\n- 3:17And the LSAT is optimized for Translation. It\'s not optimized for gist, for summary, for getting rid of the list, for only the important parts, yada, yada, yada down the line.\\n- 3:26That\'s not what Translation is. And, you know, I don\'t think that doing it will be very successful that way.\\n- 3:32And so I don\'t want you to do it that way. But it\'s kind of like in our therapist analogy, you know, if you\'re a therapist and you\'re proving that you are active, listening to your patient, your patients is speaking to you. And you\'re listening, of course, that\'s like a read stage.\\n- 3:44And then you say back to the patient, OK, what I\'m hearing from you is da da da da da da. And if you cut out a bunch of what that patient said, you know, if they said, oh, the the floral arrangements of my wedding are giving me such trouble.\\n- 3:58I don\'t know where that came from. And you just go, OK, so the arrangements for your wedding are giving you trouble and you cut out that adjective.\\n- 4:06Do you think your patients really going to think you were listening to them? They won\'t. Right. And I like in Translation to that moment when you\'re in a job interview and you have to say back, you know, you have to have some kind of like, OK, so here\'s what you want me to do.\\n- 4:21You want me to do that and say all of the stuff that you were just asked to do. All right. And now I\'m going to do it for you.\\n- 4:26You say you\'re doing some kind of job test. I know that you need to show an exhaustive understanding of the information.\\n- 4:34And one more thing about understanding very often, people will not translate the entirety of what they read. They\'ll cut out the adjectives, they\'ll cut out the details, all that different kind of stuff.\\n- 4:44And they will defend this by saying, oh, but I understood it. And and that\'s wonderful. And, you know, I respect you and that great.\\n- 4:53But if you understand it, then we need to ask, OK, is that enough? And I would say it\'s not because understanding is necessary for everything and sufficient for nothing.\\n- 5:06If you cannot use your understanding to actually go and take ideas apart, to explain ideas, to work with ideas, unpack them, look inside them, shine a spotlight around them.\\n- 5:16If you can\'t actually do that with the ideas that you\'re understanding. Well, the understanding is nice and everything, but it kind of dies on the vine. Right.\\n- 5:26I mean, it doesn\'t really get us to the place that we\'re trying to go. And so even if you do understand it, we want to move that understanding into an action stage\\n- 5:36because that action stage of actually translating it, proving that you really do have all of the information that\'s what gets us closer to getting questions right.\\n- 5:45Because if you really understand it, OK, great. Are you retaining it, though, because retention is really key to have that information that you understood so well still present with you so you can interrelated with the answer choices to know which answer is right and know which answer is wrong.\\n- 6:00And so I want you, as you\'re hearing my Translation demos, to hear how I am not cutting out information\\n- 6:08that is really, really, really, really, really important. All right. I love you. I hope this was helpful. And let\'s get to quipping.\\n\\n##### Day 2\\n\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\n\\nAnd for those who justly complain on Reddit that I already have too many acronyms in *The Loophole* 😘\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"What If I Cant Think of Anything to Say • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/what-if-i-cant-think-of-anything-to-say?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## What If I Can\'t Think of Anything to Say?\\n\\nThe moment that it\'s no longer an option to opt out\\n\\n0:05\\n\\n/\\n\\n5:32\\n\\n- 0:00What if I can\'t think of anything to say? Another classic quipping objection. You might be wondering why so many of these videos in the quipping module are about like, I don\'t want to quip.\\n- 0:10And the reason why, it\'s not that I\'m, you know, like personally against you, it\'s just I\'ve had a lot, I have a lot of baggage.\\n- 0:15I\'ve spent the past, I don\'t know, what is it? Six years, five years hearing people tell me they don\'t want to quip\\n- 0:23and, you know, giving various objections and it always comes down to like an interpersonal interaction between me and the student to finally convince them to quip.\\n- 0:32And so I\'m trying to replicate all of those interpersonal interactions, all of my best pleas, all of my like most passionate entreaties.\\n- 0:41I\'m trying to give them all to you right now in video form to replicate that style of interpersonal engagement and, you know, overcome the objections that you may have to this Methodology so that you will come with me on this journey,\\n- 0:54because I will tell you, after I overcome people\'s objections, which like I said, it\'s usually, you know, a conversation that might take a minute or two,\\n- 1:01then we really do head into a much brighter future. I even, I had one of these moments on Monday with someone where she wanted to quip,\\n- 1:09she was, you know, fine and everything. It was just that she was like, I just don\'t know what to say, which is, you know, what leads us to this video.\\n- 1:15This is something I\'ve heard a lot of times over the years, what if I can\'t think of anything to say?\\n- 1:19And I\'ll tell you the classic reasons why people think that and how you can get over it. And so the first one is like, oh, I just can\'t think of anything.\\n- 1:26You can. You can. There are a couple of reasons why you think you can\'t. One, you have some kind of internal time clock in your head for how long you wait before it\'s not worth it anymore.\\n- 1:40Because I guarantee you, if I said to you, you will not get up from this chair until you say something.\\n- 1:46I don\'t care if you sleep in this chair, you will not get up until you say something back to this piece of text that you just read.\\n- 1:53You have to have some sort of reaction to it. The moment that it\'s no longer an option to opt out because you can\'t, you\'ll find a way.\\n- 2:01Trust me, you will. The other reason why people tend to feel like, oh, I can\'t think of anything is they are amending it with I can\'t think of anything worthwhile to say.\\n- 2:11So it\'s really like, I can\'t think of anything in the amount of time I think is reasonable, or I can\'t think of anything worthwhile.\\n- 2:18And that part of worthwhile, I really wanna dive into because I don\'t want you to pre-vet your thoughts for are they good enough before you have the thought. Because the thought itself, and by thought in this case, that would be a quip on a piece of text that you just translated,\\n- 2:37by pre-vetting your thoughts, and this is beyond just the LSAT, before you actually express the thought or allow the thought to see the light of day, breathe, you are limiting your own greatness.\\n- 2:48You are limiting your own creativity. And when people say I\'m just not a creative person, it\'s probably because they are engaging in this sort of self-editing, self-vetting, being like a real Aaron Burr of a situation, say less, smile more, that we wanna say more. And smiling\\n- 3:05is good, but we wanna say more. And generally, there are no wrong answers in improv. And like I\'ve said in some of my previous videos on quipping, quipping is based on that improv mindset of you get a prompt and you just go, you just say it. There are no wrong answers. It\'s a yes and Methodology.\\n- 3:20And so when you\'re in that situation and you\'re like, oh, is this a good enough quip? Is this an okay quip? Cut that nonsense out. Just say the thing and then do it again better next time. You know, like if you say a really non-ideal quip, is the world gonna end?\\n- 3:39Am I even gonna know you did it? I won\'t, because unfortunately, we may not have a real conversation, although I would love to have a real conversation with you. But you are going to be able to go to the next sentence. And as long as you\'re pushing yourself to do better, what I care a lot more about\\n- 3:54is getting good solid reps in where you are trying your absolute best. Because remember, like I said in the previous video, there\'s no such thing as enough, there\'s only your best. And I believe that your best includes actually quipping each sentence with something substantive.\\n- 4:07If you are worried, oh, this isn\'t worthwhile, this isn\'t useful, like whatever information, you are not a good judge of what is useful before you have done the rest of the question.\\n- 4:16You do not know what will or won\'t be useful. And I would claim also that regardless of the substance of that quip that you might not think is very useful, just the habit of actually accessing your subconscious reaction to information creates usefulness by itself.\\n- 4:31Simply engaging in the habit of not questioning yourself before you have thoughts and actually just doing, rather than thinking, that by itself creates usefulness.\\n- 4:40This goes to the idea of letting the eye disappear, which is something I talk about in a different video that I\'ll link to down here. But again, one little hack you could do if you are worried about usefulness is ask a question of the piece of text you just read.\\n- 4:53And then after you\'ve asked that piece of text a question, answer the question yourself. Don\'t just pose questions, because very often that can lead to just rephrasing and putting a question mark at the end of it.\\n- 5:03But instead, if you ask a question, that\'s a great way to get your mind kickstarted, then answer that question immediately after.\\n- 5:11I hope that this helped. I hope that this unsticks some of you who think you\'re just not creative and you don\'t have anything to say.\\n- 5:16Girl, friend, person over there, you do have something to say. I know you do. I know you just need that little push out of the nest where when you say you can\'t, I simply don\'t believe you.\\n- 5:27I know you can, and so go prove me right. All right, I\'ll see you soon.\\n\\n##### Day 5\\n\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\n\\nSometimes, there are wrong answers in [improv](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C6wY9OwqJ2A).\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"9f271d","wall_time_seconds":0.2029409,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3302,"output":"---\\ntitle: \\"Wont Quipping Slow Me Down • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/wont-quipping-slow-me-down?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## Won\'t Quipping Slow Me Down?\\n\\n0:06\\n\\n/\\n\\n6:28\\n\\n- 0:00Won\'t quipping slow me down? No, no it will not. It actually speeds you up. And I know you probably think I\'m selling you a massive bill of goods right now that I am like, you know, some slimy person pounding the pavement for quipping.\\n- 0:15But really, really, really, really, really, it actually does not slow you down. It actually does speed you up. And here\'s why.\\n- 0:22I\'ll give you an analogy actually, illustrate it. So if you were, let\'s say, trying to get to an appointment on time and you\'ve got 10 minutes to navigate a mile and you are driving there, would you rather take the residential streets at the speed limit and have to go over\\n- 0:41those speed bumps and stay at a measured pace? Or would you rather speed down the business highway and have a one in 10 chance of getting pulled over?\\n- 0:49Because, and imagine you have to make this journey 25 times. Would you rather chance it where you have a one in 10 chance every time of getting pulled over?\\n- 1:01Because I wouldn\'t. I would rather just drive on the residential streets at my nice measured pace, dealing with my speed bumps, and just generally, you know, enjoying my little mini roller coaster of the residential roads,\\n- 1:12you know, the nice trees, whatever else, rather than risking getting pulled over because I\'m speeding on some kind of business highway.\\n- 1:21And so my analogy means, you know, by Parallel Reasoning, we can assume that the nice residential street with the little speed bumps, that\'s doing your actual RTQ with real quipping.\\n- 1:32Like we could say quipping is the speed bump because, oh, there\'s a cost there. Even when a quipp can take like literally one to two seconds, depending on how you quipp.\\n- 1:40I don\'t want people sitting there and be like, well, it\'s like, that\'s not good quipping. If you\'re quipping like that, it means you need to work on your RTQ and get more intuitive.\\n- 1:51But it can take seconds to quipp. So we\'ll say, you know, that\'s our speed bumps. But you know, the biggest time sink when you\'re doing the LSAT, the single, single biggest time sink, it is not knowing the answer.\\n- 2:04Not knowing the answer costs all of us a lot more time than all the virtuous process in the world. And not knowing the answer is what I liken to getting pulled over.\\n- 2:14Because if you adjust your process in such a way that it creates a lot of risk, for instance, you are, you know, just essentially moving through questions without really knowing what\'s going on.\\n- 2:25And you\'re just like, well, I just got to go. I\'ve just got to pick all that different kind of stuff.\\n- 2:29That\'s this kind of one in ten chance of getting pulled over. And the one in ten chance, you know, it could have resulted in the wrong answer.\\n- 2:34That would be also a bad outcome. It doesn\'t fit my analogy quite as well. But the analogy really fits with the idea of I\'m down to B and D and I don\'t know which one\'s right.\\n- 2:43I am now crossed off every answer and I don\'t know which one\'s right. They\'re all open. I don\'t know which one\'s right.\\n- 2:48The penalty of time that you are going to have to pay or accuracy to get yourself out of that jam, even one or two times will outweigh an entire section\'s worth of quipping.\\n- 3:01Granted, you\'ve got to be good at quipping for that math to be true. And you\'d probably be like, well, how much time have I spent?\\n- 3:07You know, let\'s play with math all we want. But just go with me on the idea here that if you get pulled over, meaning not knowing the answer, even one or two more times because you didn\'t quip, you\'re actually in the time red from declaring to quip or declining to quip.\\n- 3:23And, you know, why is that bad? And why should we pay this upfront cost instead? Well, you should pay this upfront cost because it makes you more likely to know the answer at later stages and less likely to be pulled over.\\n- 3:35I\'ll give you a couple of examples just with answer choices and also with the CLIR. So let\'s hope you\'re actually CLIRing. You know, CLIRing is great.\\n- 3:41But if you properly quip throughout the stimulus, your CLIR comes like butter so fast because you\'re actively engaging your mind in analytical operations on the text throughout your journey through the stimulus.\\n- 3:55So that CLIR is there waiting for you right at the end of the stimulus. No delay, no pause, no wondering\\n- 4:01or anything like that, because it\'s like essentially you\'ve been warming up the car the whole time that you\'ve been going through the stimulus.\\n- 4:06It\'s there ready to go because you\'ve been analyzing everything you\'ve read the whole time. I often say the best quips or function as a sort of pre CLIR. And I really believe that,\\n- 4:17like if you watch my equipping demo with A.I. Ellen, you saw that a lot of my quips were coming up as very skeptical. Right.\\n- 4:23That\'s when quip is really functioning as a pre CLIR. And so it massively speeds up that CLIRing stage because essentially you\'re working on it all throughout the stimulus.\\n- 4:30And so anytime you would have spent on the CLIR that would have been, you know, slow and sloggy and whatever else, you\'re saving by actually having quip throughout the stimulus.\\n- 4:39And so when we get to the answer choices, though, if you have a better, more engaged memory of what happened in the stimulus and you\'re able to quip the answers, you\'re more likely to be able to actually pick an answer because you\'ll be able to deduce evidence of why it is right or wrong.\\n- 4:53I know that this feels kind of like the eat your vegetables of LSAT prep, which, yes, is my specialty. But this kind of math really will work out and it\'ll also make you so much more confident that as you move forward through the question, you\'re acting based on real evidence and real knowledge,\\n- 5:11because honestly, quipping is thinking. You know, we don\'t really want to just go and willy nilly make decisions impulsively without really thinking.\\n- 5:20We want to actually take the time to think about what we\'re doing as we\'re doing it. And really, the direct content of the question is the thing we should be thinking about.\\n- 5:32And so I hope that this showed you that even if you are making an initial investment in the time it takes to quip that time it takes to quip initially, it\'s actually really not that much time. And it can be saved through the avoidance\\n- 5:48of these sorts of like time catastrophes that really do add up over the course of a section. Of course, you could retort to me that, oh, Ellen, like being down to two seconds, I\'m going to be able to do that. I would have come back at the end and all that\\n- 6:02kind of stuff. And I would say, good for you for knowing the Loophole better than almost anyone I know. Thank you.\\n- 6:08Thank you for that, Internet friend. And you know what? If you were hyper skipping, maybe that\'s true. Maybe you actually wouldn\'t burn all that time not knowing the answer. But you also probably risk getting it wrong and you would have to come back at the end.\\n- 6:20So I still think I\'m right. But thank you for that imaginary retort, imaginary Internet friend. All right. I\'m going to go ahead and move on to the next question. I think I\'m right. But thank you for that imaginary retort, imaginary Internet friend. All right. I will see you\\n- 6:25very soon on how we can do this in 35.\\n\\n##### Day 2\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"Yes You Have to Know the Lists • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/yes-you-have-to-know-the-lists?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-29\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## Yes, You Have to Know the Lists\\n\\nIt could refer to those list items by name\\n\\n0:51\\n\\n/\\n\\n4:07\\n\\n- 0:00Yes, you have to know the lists. I know I\'m the Bearer [REDACTED] bad news here, but I don\'t know why everyone thinks you don\'t have to know the lists.\\n- 0:08Because I rail on and on and on about how every word in the stimulus is the most important word in the stimulus,\\n- 0:14all of this different kind of stuff, and yet when you\'re actually sitting there vetting someone\'s Translation live, they always get to a list.\\n- 0:21And it could be as few as two items or something in a list, but especially if it gets to three, then all bets are off,\\n- 0:29and they\'ll get there and they will not translate the individual list items. And when you\'ll say to them, hey, but you know, what about the, oh, did I have to do the list?\\n- 0:38Like it somehow does not come across no matter what else I say as like, no, this is a responsibility that you have.\\n- 0:45Like you do have to know the items in a list. And I guess I would wonder why does everyone think that the items in a list are meaningless simply because there\'s more than one item in a row?\\n- 0:55You know, like it\'s really interesting because I think the brain wants to just categorize them out as like, this is a list of items or reasons or something as if they don\'t have like individual ideas to them.\\n- 1:06And lists are just words too, right? There\'s like every other word of the stimulus. They are words, just the fact that there are multiple\\n- 1:12items in a list that doesn\'t necessarily devalue any of the individual items. And so it\'s like, okay, well, but why? Why do you have to know these items in a list? Right?\\n- 1:21Because the answer choices are going to expect you to know the items in this list. That the answer choices could touch upon any of those individual list items, either individually abstracting out commonalities in the list items. It could refer to those list items by name\\n- 1:37or it could refer to them by a Translation of one of the names. And so if you don\'t really know what the list items say, well, you\'re going to just get yourself into some trouble in the answer choices, which obviously we don\'t want.\\n- 1:47And so every individual list item that you don\'t know is basically an one more opportunity that the answer choices have to potentially exploit you and cause you to get the question wrong.\\n- 1:56Obviously no one wants that. So we want you to remember the items in the list, but you might ask, okay, well, how do I remember these items that appear in lists?\\n- 2:03And this of course is for either a passage or a stimulus or even in an answer choice, like anytime you see items in an ordered list.\\n- 2:11Well, one way to remember them better is to individually intone each item with a different tone. Like for instance, if I wanted to remember apples, oranges and pears,\\n- 2:23if I said it\'s apples, oranges and pears, I can\'t say oranges, apples, oranges and pears. And I don\'t really individually designate them in my head with a different tone.\\n- 2:32It\'s a lot harder to remember apples, oranges and pears. Whereas instead, if I say apples, oranges and pears, notice that they all now sound more individualized.\\n- 2:43They have like an individual meaning put onto them. And so it\'s like, oh, why are oranges in that list? Like you can kind of hear the curiosity in the way that I said oranges and the pears, the disappointment in pears.\\n- 2:55I hate pears. I don\'t know why. I don\'t know why other people like pears, but it\'s fine. And so you can individually individually intone them and then you can individually quip list items as well.\\n- 3:06You can have those little like microquip moments after each individual list item of like apples. You know, even that little sound, that little feeling is really good at locking in your memory as I hit my glasses of that apple list item and feeling that dislike for pears, feeling the surprise\\n- 3:23with the oranges as you read it. All of those individual kind of microquips are going to do wonders for the memory to help you lock in that meaning.\\n- 3:31So if you\'re engaging with them on an individual basis, you\'re intoning them on an individual basis and you translate them individually when you do the Translation step, it\'s going to be a lot, a lot easier to remember those list items. But honestly, the biggest thing that helps with remembering\\n- 3:45a list is just accepting that like they are not less important. They are just as important as every other part of the stimulus. And so as soon as you elevate them to the level of like they\'re important, they are like they\'re they\'re good. I have to know them. These are not optional. Often\\n- 4:01the issue goes away with them just to start. All right. I hope that that helps. It\'s a little micro video and I will see you soon.\\n\\n##### Day 5\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.4 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"9a31ed","wall_time_seconds":0.244443,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":5974,"output":"---\\ntitle: \\"Quip Time to Clap Back • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/quip-time-to-clap-back?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## Quip: Time to Clap Back 💅🏽\\n\\n0:11\\n\\n/\\n\\n10:14\\n\\n- 0:00Ah, quipping. This is the time you clap back. You actually say something back to the author inside the stimulus and tell them what you think.\\n- 0:13You know, you just read a piece of text, you translated it, now you\'re in the third part of the RQTQ process, which you\'re going to run on every chunk of text you enCounter on the L-SAT\\n- 0:22and it is now your job to get actively engaged and say something back to the author about what they just read.\\n- 0:29And so this part of the process, it\'s a bit open-ended, right? Because saying something back, having an independent thought about what you just read, having a reaction, there are lots of ways to have reactions, right?\\n- 0:44But there are going to be some kinds of reactions that are more useful than other kinds of reactions.\\n- 0:50And so I will outline for you here just some quick tips of things to avoid, things to lean on as we get into our quipping journey\\n- 0:58and then there are going to be lots more videos just devoted to quipping very soon. And so if you get all of your questions not quite answered here, don\'t worry,\\n- 1:04there is more content where this came from. And so in this quipping stage, you know, I have now just internalized what I read.\\n- 1:09You know, I\'ve set it back to myself of like, I understand it, I\'m able to retain it. And now in the quipped stage, this is when we\'re going to work with what we read.\\n- 1:18We\'re going to view it through the lens of our beautiful brains, all of our experiences, all of our long-term memories, our previous knowledge.\\n- 1:25And we\'re going to be like, hmm, we\'re going to get a little bit curious and we\'re going to go on a bit of an investigation.\\n- 1:33The best sorts of quips are investigative. They\'re necessarily skeptical, but they\'re more like, hmm, did that add up, everyone?\\n- 1:43Because they investigate relationships between pieces of text. And you might be objecting to me, well, if I only have one piece of text that I just have to be able to get to the bottom of it. How will I ever quip it?\\n- 1:56Because it\'s only one thing. It doesn\'t have a relationship to anything else yet. Well, for that one, you could just have a reaction to the information.\\n- 2:02You can make some kind of joke. You could come up with an example. You could laugh about it. And like, honestly, like an emotion is a quip because an emotion is a reaction.\\n- 2:13You only have emotions about things that you actually understand, which leads me to what the genesis of quipping was.\\n- 2:20I think it\'s kind of useful for you to understand why quipping exists so that you can do it most effectively.\\n- 2:26Quipping exists because I was watching someone translate in 2019 and I heard him. I heard the words as he intoned his Translation. There was just no music there.\\n- 2:34You know, it was I likened it to an empty cardboard box. Like every word was an empty cardboard box. And you knew that he was saying the word, but he had no idea what was inside that cardboard box.\\n- 2:45He just didn\'t really understand the significance of any of the words he was saying. Even if he was saying the right words, you could hear that they were empty.\\n- 2:52And so I stopped him and I told him to go back and I was just like, OK, yeah, yeah, this time, you know, you\'re saying everything right.\\n- 2:58Just do one more thing for me of I want you to read and I want you to translate. And this time I want you to say something about what you just read after you\'ve read it.\\n- 3:06And he was like, well, what do you want me to say? I was like, well, it could be anything. Just something about what you read.\\n- 3:09Just have a feeling, have a thought, have a critique, whatever you want to say. And he did that. And the magic thing that happened was not that, you know, his quip was so genius.\\n- 3:19It was that the empty cardboard box feeling of the Translation went away because by having to know what the Translation actually meant in order to quip, then he was forced to understand what the Translation really meant on a deep level,\\n- 3:37because essentially the brain is going to optimize away anything that isn\'t immediately used in a process as superfluous, not really needed. And it\'s going to find the lowest common denominator, the least effort version of everything.\\n- 3:48And so it\'ll be like, oh, I\'m saying the words of this Translation, but unless I\'m actually going to do something with those words very, very quickly, again, die on the vine sort of thing where I will technically pantomime the correct words, but they won\'t get us where we\'re trying to go\\n- 4:03by making you come up with an independent thought about what you just read. We ensure that that Translation is moving towards the right direction, which is really understanding real nuanced information.\\n- 4:14And so why are these quips so useful, though, just in and of themselves beyond the impact they have on the quality of the Translation stage?\\n- 4:22It\'s because our memory indexes on relationships like I\'ll give you one example to show you what I mean here.\\n- 4:30I want you to memorize four, four, four, four, four, four, four, four. Now I want you to memorize eight, four, six, two, zero, nine, eight, four. One of these is a little bit easier to memorize, right?\\n- 4:47And you might be like, yeah, because they\'re all the same number. Come on, you give a Counter in your fingers.\\n- 4:51It was nine, four. So yeah, you\'re right. I did count on my fingers. But the reason why all those fours were easier to remember is because each digit had a relationship to each other.\\n- 5:06And what you did was you just memorized the relationship. You didn\'t memorize the individual digits. You memorize nine fours as opposed to\\n- 5:13eight four, dot, dot, dot, dot, down the line. And that is what equipped us to a certain extent.\\n- 5:19It leverages the information to make us intergauge with the relationships and the relationships are the real takeaways that work in our memory to glue information together.\\n- 5:31Not only does it uplift the quality of the Translation, it uplifts the quality of your memory because neurons that fire together wire together\\n- 5:39when you bring your lifetime of information in your long term memory to what you read in that quip in your reaction of like, oh, you know, actually, we don\'t always operate on the same schedule because whatever. I don\'t know what you\'re going to say.\\n- 5:54You are then activating all of your long term memories about railroads and you\'re doing it in the same context as these, you know, the new piece of information in your working memory about this timetable in Shoeville that\'s in the stimulus.\\n- 6:07And now these two pieces of the brain are firing together, meaning they\'re going to wire together. And now that piece of information about the train timetable in Shoeville has a much higher chance of being retained further on because you\'ll remember the relationship\\n- 6:25to all of your other information that you have about trains in your life. And it\'s really funny because as we get into some of the more advanced drills like M.A.C., this is where I really, really learned about the power of creating a relationship in your quip to enhance memory,\\n- 6:43because when we get into some of the harder memory drills, man, will you ever see how important those\\n- 6:49quips really are to enhance your memory? When memory gets hard, that\'s when quipping gets more and more impactful because it is the way that you can actually encode and index all of that information much more thoughtfully to create a much easier memory experience.\\n- 7:05I was teaching Translation and quipping to a friend who is not an LSAT student. He was just a person I know who wanted to learn about Translation and quipping\\n- 7:13for his own reading of like books he liked to read. And when I explained it to him, he was like, OK, Ellen, now I get it.\\n- 7:20He\'s like, I was like, what? He was like, you don\'t teach better compression. You teach better indexing. You don\'t want people to have less information to reduce the amount of information. You want them to index it better.\\n- 7:32That\'s why you have this whole quipping thing is to create the indexed relationships between information. I was like, dang, you\'re really smart.\\n- 7:39I wish I could go up with that myself. And he was right. That is exactly what this is. It\'s creating indexes between the information to enhance your retention.\\n- 7:47But now, like, there\'s even one more thing to see in this video. Give me one more benefit of quipping that I want to show you how it works. There\'s\\n- 7:55one one prime objection to quipping is people will often say, oh, well, I\'m just not creative. I don\'t know what to say.\\n- 8:02And if you\'re in that situation where you\'re just not creative and you don\'t know what to say, I will tell you, creativity is a muscle that you can hold.\\n- 8:10And what clipping is, is it\'s shower thoughts on steroids. You know, when you\'re in the shower and it\'s quiet and you\'re just washing your hair or something and all of a sudden thoughts will just kind of appear to you and you aren\'t willing them\\n- 8:25into existence, they\'re just going there because the brain isn\'t occupied with other conscious thoughts. That\'s when your subconscious gets to come into play.\\n- 8:32And when you\'re in that quip stage, you get used to consistently asking your subconscious for output. I don\'t get to tell you exactly what, you know, what output, but I want output.\\n- 8:44I want a reaction. Tell me what my reaction is. And your subconscious mind, then, by being asked for those shower thoughts on steroids on a reliable schedule, it knows when it\'s going to get asked for its next shower thought of like your reaction to what you just read.\\n- 8:57That\'s the quip. Then it gets a lot more used to the responsibility of being creative on command. That those moments that just happen, they just happen because you grease the wheels enough times to make it muscle memory of your, your body will\\n- 9:13do the thing that you train it to do. And I will just tell you one, you know, one group of people that has these sorts of aha creativity moments really quickly, like we do with quipping,\\n- 9:26they\'re improvisers. If you ever watched, you know, that old show like whose line is it anyway, or gone to an improv show or something like that, you know that they\'re making up the skit as they go, right?\\n- 9:36Like in HBO Silicon Valley, which fans of the Loophole would know that I love, they actually improv to time, especially Zach Woods, obsessed with Zach Woods, but they will make up a lot of the show right in the middle of it. And they have this rule of yes\\n- 9:47and you\'re never allowed to say no. It may surprise you to learn I was an improviser since I was five years old.\\n- 9:54And that\'s why I\'m so used to just coming up with something in the moment. When you ask your brain to respond every single time you input information,\\n- 10:02which is what you do when you quip, you will become exponentially more creative, build so many stronger muscles because you ask yourself to. You can do it.\\n- 10:12I believe in you. All right. I\'ll see you soon.\\n\\n##### Day 2\\n\\n### Ellen\'s Notes\\n\\nAnd for those who justly complain on Reddit that I already have too many acronyms in *The Loophole* 😘\\n\\n### Take Notes\\n\\n### The Expanded Universe\\n\\n## Video Course Review\\n\\nConcept Review\\r\\n---\\ntitle: \\"Whats Quipping Lets Try It Together • LO\\"\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/whats-quipping-lets-try-it-together?source=timeline&plan=foundation-plan\\"\\nauthor:\\npublished:\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\ntags:\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\n---\\n## What\'s Quipping? Let\'s Try It Together\\n\\n0:07\\n\\n/\\n\\n9:34\\n\\n- 0:00Alright, pause the video and just read the thing that\'s on the screen because I know you\'re gonna want to read it and I want you to read it.\\n- 0:05Alright, did you pause? Did you pause? Okay, good. I\'m assuming you paused and you\'ve read what\'s on the screen. You\'re like, what the heck is this?\\n- 0:15Well, this is AI Ellen. I have been training an AI doppelganger version of me and so you can see that I\'ve recruited her to the enterprise.\\n- 0:22The reason I\'ve recruited AI Ellen to the enterprise today is because I want to actually have real real-time Improv\'d quipping that you know I didn\'t prepare for because I didn\'t write the example.\\n- 0:34AI Ellen wrote the example. And so I am going to be going through it and doing a bunch of quickfire quipping demos with you right now that you can do along with me.\\n- 0:41But one thing before we get started is make sure before you watch this video you have watched the Translation 2.0 module\\n- 0:48and especially the video on quipping in the Translation 2.0 module because there\'s a lot of intro and material that I\'m not gonna go over in this video.\\n- 0:54Instead, we\'re gonna work from that base and do a bunch of quickfire examples together. And so go watch a video if you haven\'t already.\\n- 1:01And as I get example sentences here from AI Ellen, I want you to as soon as it gets generated also pause the video and try and translate and quipp that sentence yourself before you listen to me do it.\\n- 1:12That way it\'ll be more fun. You\'ll be able to be more engaged and interactive with what we\'re gonna be doing together. And who knows what\'s going to happen?\\n- 1:19And so as we go, I\'m just going to press the number one and AI Ellen is going to give us a sentence in a vacuum.\\n- 1:26And it\'s gonna be my job to translate and quipp that sentence for you right now real time live. And so you might be asking, okay, well, why aren\'t you doing this with a real LSAT stimulus?\\n- 1:34You know, why is it one sentence at a time? Stimuli are long. Economic growth was even stronger this year and so then it\'s like, yeah, but did we change our policies, right? That\'s kind of a relevant question. So economist, there was nothing at all bumbling\\n- 0:33about my warning. Okay, so my warning was great. It wasn\'t bumbling at all and it\'s like, okay, maybe because like the question of whether or not we actually changed the policies would bear there. So indeed, it convinced the country\'s leaders to change economic policies which is what prevented a\\n- 0:47recession.
